k_{qY},\,k_{eY}

Table 1:
Field content and symmetry assignments in the quark and lepton
sectors. Here
Y_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}^{(k_{qY})}(\tau_{q})

and
Y_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}^{(k_{eY})}(\tau_{e})
denote modular forms of weights

k_{qY}
and
k_{eY}
transforming in
A_{4}
representations

\mathbf{r}_{q}
and
\mathbf{r}_{e}
in the quark and lepton sectors.

Under a modular transformation with

\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in A_{4}
,
the modular forms
Y_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}^{(k_{qY})}(\tau_{q})
and

Y_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}^{(k_{eY})}(\tau_{e})
appearing in the last column of
Table
1
transform as

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}^{(k_{qY})}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\;(c\tau_{q}+d)^{k_{qY}}\,\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}(\gamma)\,Y_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}^{(k_{qY})}(\tau_{q})\,,

(2.77)

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}^{(k_{eY})}(\tau_{e})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\;(c\tau_{e}+d)^{k_{eY}}\,\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}(\gamma)\,Y_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}^{(k_{eY})}(\tau_{e})\,.

Modular invariance of the Lagrangian requires that, for each interaction term,
the tensor product of the
A_{4}
representations carried by the matter fields
and by the corresponding modular form contains the trivial representation.
Concretely, in the quark sector one demands

\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q_{1}}}\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q_{2}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q_{n}}}\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}\supset\mathbf{1}\,,

(2.78)

where
\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{q_{i}}}
denotes the
A_{4}
representation matrix of the quark field

q_{i}
appearing in the operator. Similarly, in the lepton sector one requires

\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{\ell_{1}}}\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{\ell_{2}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{\ell_{m}}}\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}\supset\mathbf{1}\,,

(2.79)

where
\rho_{\mathbf{r}_{\ell_{j}}}
denotes the
A_{4}
representation matrix of the lepton field

\ell_{j}
appearing in the operator.

The modular weights are constrained in the same way: for each quark-sector operator,
the sum of the modular weights of the quark fields must match the weight of the associated modular form,

\sum_{i\in\text{quarks}}k_{q_{i}}=k_{qY}\,,

(2.80)

while in the lepton sector one has

\sum_{j\in\text{leptons}}k_{\ell_{j}}=k_{eY}\,.

(2.81)

These conditions ensure that all Yukawa terms constructed from the field content in
Table
1
are invariant under the modular symmetry.

For the quark sector, the left-handed doublets are assembled into an
A_{4}
triplet,

Q_{L}=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{1}\\[2.0pt]
Q_{2}\\[2.0pt]
Q_{3}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\overline{Q}_{L}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{Q}_{1}\\[2.0pt]
\overline{Q}_{3}\\[2.0pt]
\overline{Q}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad Q_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}u_{L}\\
d_{L}\end{pmatrix},\quad Q_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}c_{L}\\
s_{L}\end{pmatrix},\quad Q_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}t_{L}\\
b_{L}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.82)

The right-handed quarks are taken to be
A_{4}
singlets with assignments

(u_{R}^{c},\,c_{R}^{c},\,t_{R}^{c})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}}),\quad(u_{R},\,c_{R},\,t_{R})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}),

(2.83)

(d_{R}^{c},\,s_{R}^{c},\,b_{R}^{c})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}}),\quad(d_{R},\,s_{R},\,b_{R})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}})\,.

(2.84)

Analogously, in the lepton sector,

L_{L}=\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}\\[2.0pt]
L_{2}\\[2.0pt]
L_{3}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\overline{L}_{L}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{L}_{1}\\[2.0pt]
\overline{L}_{3}\\[2.0pt]
\overline{L}_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad L_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{eL}\\
e_{L}\end{pmatrix},\quad L_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{\mu L}\\
\mu_{L}\end{pmatrix},\quad L_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{\tau L}\\
\tau_{L}\end{pmatrix},

(2.85)

and

(e_{R}^{c},\,\mu_{R}^{c},\,\tau_{R}^{c})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}}),\qquad(e_{R},\,\mu_{R},\,\tau_{R})=(\mathbf{1},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\,\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}})\,.

(2.86)

Within this model, the Yukawa sector can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{u}\,u^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3a}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\alpha^{\prime}_{u}\,u^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3b}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\beta_{u}\,c^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3a}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\beta^{\prime}_{u}\,c^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3b}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma_{u}\,t^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3a}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\gamma^{\prime}_{u}\,t^{c}\,\tilde{H}^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3b}}^{(6)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+{\rm h.c.},

(2.87)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{d}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{d}\,d^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\beta_{d}\,s^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+\gamma_{d}\,b^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{q})\,Q+{\rm h.c.},

(2.88)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{e}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{e}\,e^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{e})\,L+\beta_{e}\,\mu^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{e})\,L+\gamma_{e}\,\tau^{c}\,H^{\dagger}\,\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{e})\,L+{\rm h.c.}\,.

(2.89)

In this Yukawa setup, there are in total
28
real free parameters.
They consist of
12
complex Yukawa coefficients,

\alpha_{u},\alpha^{\prime}_{u},\beta_{u},\beta^{\prime}_{u},\gamma_{u},\gamma^{\prime}_{u}
in the up-quark sector,

\alpha_{d},\beta_{d},\gamma_{d}
in the down-quark sector, and

\alpha_{e},\beta_{e},\gamma_{e}
in the lepton sector, together with two complex moduli

\tau_{q}
and
\tau_{e}
, which contribute
4
additional real parameters.

Once the fields and their representations are fixed as in Table
1
, the original basis symmetry
U(3)^{5}
is no longer preserved.
In particular, for the left-handed multiplets the
U(3)
symmetry is reduced to a single
U(1)
phase rotation, while for the right-handed fields the
U(3)
acting on the three generations is broken down to independent
U(1)
symmetries for each flavor.
Consequently, the initial
U(3)^{5}
symmetry is reduced to a product of
11

U(1)
factors, so that there are
11
independent
U(1)
phases in total.

\begin{array}[]{c}U(3)_{q}\\
U(3)_{\ell}\\
U(3)_{u}\\
U(3)_{d}\\
U(3)_{e}\end{array}\xrightarrow{\;\;\;\;\;\;A_{4}\;\;\text{modular symmetry}\;\;\;\;\;\;}\begin{array}[]{c}U(1)_{q}\\
U(1)_{\ell}\\
U(1)_{u}\times U(1)_{c}\times U(1)_{t}\\
U(1)_{d}\times U(1)_{s}\times U(1)_{b}\\
U(1)_{e}\times U(1)_{\mu}\times U(1)_{\tau}\end{array}

(2.90)

Figure 1:
U(3)^{5}
is broken down to
U(1)^{11}
by the
A_{4}
modular symmetry.

Among these, the combinations corresponding to baryon number
U(1)_{B}
and lepton number
U(1)_{L}
still leave the leading-order Yukawa sector invariant, and thus do not reduce the number of parameters.
Consequently, there are
11-2=9
independent rephasings that can be used to remove unphysical phases, leaving

28-9=19

(2.91)

independent real parameters in the modular Yukawa sector.
More concretely, the quark sector contains
14
independent parameters, while the lepton sector contains
5
.

Under our minimal assumption that the leading-order Yukawa sector is the sole origin of flavor structure, we do not include the kinetic term of
\tau
nor the effects of
\mathrm{Im}\,\tau
on higher-dimensional operators; instead, we treat all Yukawa-sector modular forms as building blocks.
Since any holomorphic modular form can be generated from the triplet
Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}
via symmetric tensor powers, it is sufficient to take
Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{q})
and
Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}(\tau_{e})
as the elementary building blocks.
When the modular form
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau)
is treated as a spurion field and used as a building block, the flavor-breaking sources of higher-dimensional operators are correspondingly restricted.
In this way, the flavor symmetry is broken uniquely by two moduli
\tau_{q}
and
\tau_{e}
when considering
A_{4}^{(q)}\otimes A_{4}^{(e)}
.
This procedure is therefore MFV-like.
Building on the modular-symmetric Standard Model together with the
A_{4}
tensor algebra in Appendix
A.1
, we then employ the Hilbert series method to construct general SMEFT operators that are invariant under the
A_{4}
modular symmetry.

3
Hilbert series method for
A_{4}
modular symmetry

In this section, we introduce the Hilbert series. We first discuss the Hilbert series for compact continuous groups, and then present the corresponding structure for discrete groups. Moreover, taking the fundamental modular form
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
as the basic building block, we derive the Hilbert series that determines the number of independent operators.

3.1
Hilbert series for continuous group

Given a set of multiplets
q_{i}
transforming in representations

R_{i}
of a (compact) group
G
, the Hilbert series is defined as
the generating function

H(q_{i})\;=\;\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{N}=0}^{\infty}c_{k_{1},\dots,k_{N}}\,q_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots q_{N}^{k_{N}},

(3.1)

where the coefficients
c_{k_{1},\dots,k_{N}}
are the multiplicities of the trivial representation in the tensor product
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{i}}R_{i}
with
\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{i}}R_{i}
denoting the
k_{i}
-order symmetric tensor product of
R_{i}
.

For a compact group
G
, the Hilbert series can be obtained using the integration formula,

H(q_{i})=\oint d\mu(z)\mathrm{PE}\!\left[q_{i}\,\chi_{q_{i}}(z)\right]

(3.2)

where
z
is the maximal torus variable of the group
G
, and
d\mu(z)
,
\chi_{q_{i}}(z)
are the integral measure and character expressed by them, respectively. The symmetrized products of the characters are generated by the plethystic exponential (PE)

\mathrm{PE}\!\left[q_{i}\,\chi_{q_{i}}(z)\right]\;\equiv\;\exp\!\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q_{i}^{\,n}}{n}\,\chi_{q_{i}}(z^{n})\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q_{i}^{\,n}\,\chi_{\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(q_{i})}(z)\,,

(3.3)

Because of the orthogonality of the group characters,
such an integral extracts the multiplicity of the trivial representation, which counts the number of operators.
In effective field theory (EFT), we treat all fields
\phi_{i}
and the covariant derivative
D_{\mu}
as group multiplets.
Truncating at a certain mass order, the Hilbert series presents both the number and field contents of the operators.

The derivative is a special building block since it raises two redundancies of the operators, equation of motion (EOM) and integration by part (IBP). To eliminate these redundancies, the Lorentz group should be extended to the conformal group. In the conformal group, the derivative is a lower operator of its Cartan sub-algebra, so that for each field
\phi
, its conformal representation takes the form

\phi\rightarrow R_{\phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi\\[2.0pt]
D_{\mu}\phi\\[2.0pt]
D_{(\mu_{1}}D_{\mu_{2})}\phi\\[2.0pt]
\vdots\\[2.0pt]
D_{(\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n})}\phi\\[2.0pt]
\vdots\end{pmatrix},

(3.4)

where
D_{(\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{n})}
denotes the totally symmetrized combination of derivatives.
This defines the single-particle module
(SPM)
R_{\phi}

[
11
]
.
The SPM character is defined as

\chi_{\phi}(D,z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D^{n}\,\chi_{\mathrm{sym}^{n}(D)}(z)\,\chi_{\phi}(z),

(3.5)

where
D
is the spurion corresponding to the covariant derivative formal,
\chi_{\phi}(g)
is the character of the Lorentz representation of
\phi
, and
\chi_{\mathrm{sym}^{n}(D)}(z)
denotes the character of the rank-
n
symmetric tensor representation of the derivatives.

For the multi-particle sector, we can introduce generating functions for symmetric and antisymmetric tensor products. For a bosonic field

\phi
, the
n
-particle character corresponds to the symmetric tensor product
\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(\phi)
of the SPM. While for the fermionic field, the
n
-particle character corresponds to the antisymmetric tensor product. Thus, the PE is generalized to
[
99
]

\displaystyle\text{boson:}\quad\mathrm{PE}\!\left[\phi\,\chi_{\phi}(D,z)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\;\exp\!\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\phi^{\,n}}{n}\,\chi_{\phi}(D^{n},z^{n})\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\phi^{\,n}\,\chi_{\mathrm{Sym}^{n}(\phi)}(D,z)\,,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\text{fermion:}\quad\mathrm{PEF}\!\left[\phi\,\chi_{\phi}(D,z)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\;\exp\!\left[-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-\phi)^{n}}{n}\,\chi_{\phi}(D^{n},z^{n})\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\phi^{\,n}\,\chi_{\wedge^{n}(\phi)}(D,z)\,,

where
\chi_{\wedge^{n}(\phi)}(D,z)
denotes the antisymmetric product of the characters.
For a theory with several distinct fields, the multi-particle generating function is obtained by multiplying together the PEs and PEFs of each field.

When considering the EOM, the SPM
\phi
for a scalar field should be reduced by removing all components that
are proportional to the scalar EOM
(D^{2}+m^{2})\phi=0
. Under this constraint, the character can be written as

\chi_{\phi}(D,z)\rightarrow\chi_{\phi}(D,z)-D^{2}\chi(D,z)=(1-D^{2})\,P(D,z)\,\chi_{(0,0)}(z)\,.

(3.7)

Here,
\chi_{(0,0)}
denote the character of the Lorentz representation, and
P(D,z)
denotes the character of the symmetrical derivatives tensor product as

P(D,z)\equiv\frac{1}{(1-Dz_{1})(1-D/z_{1})(1-Dz_{2})(1-D/z_{2})}\,.

(3.8)

For a left-handed fermion, the decomposition of
\mathrm{sym}^{n}(D)\psi_{L}
takes the form

\mathrm{sym}^{n}\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\otimes\left(\tfrac{1}{2},0\right)=\left(\tfrac{n+1}{2},\,\tfrac{n}{2}\right)\oplus\cdots.

(3.9)

Here only the highest–weight component
\left(\tfrac{n+1}{2},\,\tfrac{n}{2}\right)
is kept. Taking such constraint into account, the character of the fermionic module

R_{\psi_{L}}
becomes

\chi_{{\psi_{L}}}(D,z)=\Bigl(\chi_{(\tfrac{1}{2},0)}(z)-D\,\chi_{(0,\tfrac{1}{2})}(z)\Bigr)\,P(D,z)\,.

(3.10)

For the left-handed field strength
F_{L}
with
n
derivatives, the redundancies

D_{\mu}F_{L}^{\mu\nu}=D_{\mu}D_{\nu}F^{\mu\nu}_{L}=0\,,

(3.11)

imply the decomposition

\chi_{{F_{L}}}(D,z)=\Bigl(\chi_{(1,0)}(D,z)-D\,\chi_{(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})}(D,z)+D^{2}\chi_{(0,0)}(D,z)\Bigr)\,P(D,z).

(3.12)

IBP redundancy means that if one operator differs from another only by a total derivative, then these two operators are physically equivalent, because the total derivative becomes a boundary term and does not change the action after integrating the Lagrangian over space–time. By considering this redundancy, the Hilbert series can finally be represented as:

H(D,\phi)=\int d\mu_{L}(z)\,\frac{1}{P(D,z)}\,\mathrm{PE}\!\left(\phi\,\chi_{\phi}(D,z)\right)+\Delta H\,,

(3.13)

where
\Delta H
only receives contributions from operators with
mass-dimension below four and will therefore be ignored, and
d\mu_{L}
denotes
the Haar measure of the Lorentz group
SO(4,\mathbb{C})
.

In an EFT, the symmetry group is typically taken to be

G=SO(4)\times I,

(3.14)

where
SO(4)
is the Lorentz group and
I
is the
internal symmetry group.
In this case, the full Hilbert series can
be written in the form

H(D,\{\phi_{i}\})=\int d\mu_{SO(4)}(z)\,d\mu_{I}(z)\,\frac{1}{P(D,z)}\,\prod_{i}\begin{cases}\mathrm{PE}\!\big[\phi_{i}\,\chi_{\phi_{i}}(D,z,a)\big],&\phi_{i}\ \text{bosonic},\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{PEF}\!\big[\phi_{i}\,\chi_{\phi_{i}}(D,z,a)\big],&\phi_{i}\ \text{fermionic}.\end{cases}

(3.15)

As an example, in SMEFT, the symmetry group is

G_{\text{SMEFT}}\;=\;SO(4)\times SU(3)_{c}\times SU(2)_{L}\times U(1)_{Y}\,,

(3.16)

where
SO(4)
is the Euclidean Lorentz group and the remaining
factors form the Standard Model gauge group. We use maximal-torus
variables
z=(z_{1},z_{2})
for
SO(4)
, and collectively denote by

a
the maximal-torus variables of the internal gauge group

SU(3)_{c}\times SU(2)_{L}\times U(1)_{Y}
.
The SMEFT field content includes the Higgs doublet
H
, the
left-handed quark and lepton doublets
Q
and
L
, the
right-handed singlets
u^{c},d^{c},e^{c}
, and the gauge field strengths

G_{\mu\nu},W_{\mu\nu},B_{\mu\nu}
. For each of these fields

\phi_{i}
, we construct an EOM-reduced single-particle module

R_{\phi_{i}}
as in the general discussion, and define its
single-particle character

\chi_{\phi_{i}}(D,z,a)\,,

(3.17)

which factorizes into a Lorentz part (built from the appropriate
scalar, spinor, or field-strength SPM with EOM already removed) and an
internal part encoding the corresponding

\big(SU(3)_{c},SU(2)_{L},U(1)_{Y}\big)
representation of
\phi_{i}
.

The SMEFT Hilbert series follows by applying the general formula for

G=SO(4)\times I
to the SMEFT group. Explicitly,

H_{\text{SMEFT}}(D,\{\phi_{i}\})=\int d\mu_{SO(4)}(z)\,d\mu_{SU(3)_{c}\times SU(2)_{L}\times U(1)_{Y}}(a)\,\frac{1}{P(D,z)}\,\prod_{i}\begin{cases}\mathrm{PE}\!\big[\phi_{i}\,\chi_{{\phi_{i}}}(D,z,a)\big],&\phi_{i}\ \text{bosonic},\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{PEF}\!\big[\phi_{i}\,\chi_{{\phi_{i}}}(D,z,a)\big],&\phi_{i}\ \text{fermionic}.\end{cases}

(3.18)

To obtain the spectrum of SMEFT operators at fixed mass dimension

d
, we rescale

D\;\to\;\varepsilon\,D\,,\qquad\phi_{i}\;\to\;\varepsilon^{d_{\phi_{i}}}\,\phi_{i}\,,

(3.19)

where
d_{\phi_{i}}
is the canonical mass dimension of the field

\phi_{i}
, and expand the SMEFT Hilbert series in powers of

\varepsilon
. The coefficient of
\varepsilon^{d}
then gives the
number and field contents of independent SMEFT operators at mass dimension

d
, with the IBP and EOM removed.

3.2
Hilbert series for discrete group

The Hilbert series applies not only to compact Lie groups but also to finite discrete groups. Consider a finite group
G
with
|G|
elements. The character
\chi
is a class function, which is constant on each conjugacy class
C_{i}\subset G
and can be written as
\chi(C_{i})
. If the class
C_{i}
contains
n_{i}
elements, the orthogonality relation for characters reads

\langle\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{i}n_{i}\,\chi_{1}(C_{i})\,\chi_{2}^{*}(C_{i}).

(3.20)

Based on the inner product, we define the multiplicity n of the irreducible representation
R
as:

n=\langle\chi,\chi_{R}\rangle.

(3.21)

If
\chi_{R}=1
, then the multiplicity
n
gives the number of independent operators.

\begin{array}[]{c|cccc}&C_{1}&C_{2}&C_{3}&C_{4}\\
\hline\cr\text{class rep.}&e&{T^{2}}&{T}&{S}\\
\text{class size}&1&4&4&3\\
\hline\cr\chi^{(\mathbf{1})}&1&1&1&1\\
\chi^{(\mathbf{1^{\prime}})}&1&\omega&\omega^{2}&1\\
\chi^{(\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}})}&1&\omega^{2}&\omega&1\\
\chi^{(\mathbf{3})}&3&0&0&-1\end{array}

Table 2:
Character table of the alternating group
A_{4}
.
C_{1}
is the identity class,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
are the two conjugacy classes of
3
-cycles, and
C_{4}
is the class of double transpositions. The irreducible representations are three singlets
\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
,
\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
and one triplet
\mathbf{3}
. Here
\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}
.

For the tensor-product representation
q_{1}\otimes q_{2}
, the character satisfies

\chi_{q_{1}\otimes q_{2}}(C_{i})=\chi_{q_{1}}(C_{i})\,\chi_{q_{2}}(C_{i})\,.

(3.22)

When
q_{1}=q_{2}\equiv q
, the product representation
q\otimes q
can be decomposed into its symmetric and antisymmetric parts.
For instance, for a three-dimensional representation with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
,
\lambda_{3}
of a representative group element in the class
C_{i}
, the symmetric and antisymmetric characters can be written as

\chi_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}}(C_{i})=\lambda_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\,,

(3.23)

\chi_{\wedge^{2}}(C_{i})=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\,.

(3.24)

In general, suppose there are
n
identical terms, and for each term the eigenvalues are
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}
.
The corresponding generating functions are given by plethystic exponentials.
The symmetric and antisymmetric generating functions are

\text{PE}(q;\lambda(g))=\exp\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{r}\,\lambda_{i}^{r}(g)}{r}\right),

(3.25)

\text{PEF}(q;\lambda(g))=\exp\!\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-q)^{r}\,\lambda_{i}^{r}(g)}{r}\right).

(3.26)

Each
q^{m}
term in the expansion of these generating functions corresponds to the character of the (anti)symmetric representation at order
m
.

For a single variable
q
, the Hilbert series can be written as

H(q)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\begin{cases}\text{PE}(q;\lambda(g)),&\text{if }q\text{ is bosonic},\\
\text{PEF}(q;\lambda(g)),&\text{if }q\text{ is fermionic}.\end{cases}

(3.27)

For several multiplets
q_{i}
, the Hilbert series naturally takes the form

H(q_{1},\dots,q_{i})=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\prod_{i}\begin{cases}\text{PE}(q_{i};\lambda(g)),&\text{if }q_{i}\text{ is bosonic},\\
\text{PEF}(q_{i};\lambda(g)),&\text{if }q_{i}\text{ is fermionic}.\end{cases}

(3.28)

As an example, we consider two distinct
A_{4}
triplets, denoted by
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
The relevant
A_{4}
representations are given in Table
2
.
Using the three-dimensional matrix representation of the
A_{4}
group elements summarized in Appendix
A.1
, we write the triplet character as

\chi_{\mathbf{3}}(g)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\lambda_{k}(g),\qquad g\in A_{4},

(3.29)

where
\lambda_{k}(g)
are the eigenvalues of the representation matrix.
Then, we can then write the corresponding plethystic exponential as

\text{PE}(T_{1},T_{2};\lambda(g))=\exp\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{1}^{r}\lambda_{i}^{r}(g)}{r}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{2}^{r}\lambda_{i}^{r}(g)}{r}\right).

(3.30)

Using Eq. (
3.28
), we obtain the Hilbert series

H(T_{1},T_{2})=\frac{T_{1}^{4}\left(T_{2}^{4}-T_{2}^{2}+1\right)+T_{1}^{3}T_{2}\left(T_{2}^{2}+T_{2}+1\right)-T_{1}^{2}(T_{2}+1)^{2}\left(T_{2}^{2}-3T_{2}+1\right)+T_{1}T_{2}\left(T_{2}^{2}+T_{2}+1\right)+T_{2}^{4}-T_{2}^{2}+1}{(1-T_{1}^{2})^{2}(1-T_{2}^{2})^{2}(1-T_{1}^{3})(1-T_{2}^{3})}\,.

(3.31)

Expanding in the total degree of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, we obtain the multiplicity of invariant singlets at each tensor-product order,

\displaystyle H(T_{1},T_{2})=1

\displaystyle+\bigl(T_{1}^{2}+T_{1}T_{2}+T_{2}^{2}\bigr)

(3.32)

\displaystyle+\bigl(T_{1}^{3}+T_{1}^{2}T_{2}+T_{1}T_{2}^{2}+T_{2}^{3}\bigr)

\displaystyle+\bigl(2T_{1}^{4}+3T_{1}^{3}T_{2}+4T_{1}^{2}T_{2}^{2}+3T_{1}T_{2}^{3}+2T_{2}^{4}\bigr)

\displaystyle+\bigl(T_{1}^{5}+3T_{1}^{4}T_{2}+4T_{1}^{3}T_{2}^{2}+4T_{1}^{2}T_{2}^{3}+3T_{1}T_{2}^{4}+T_{2}^{5}\bigr)+O\!\left(6\right).

As a quick check, we consider the trivial singlet structures in
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}
.
Comparing with the tensor-product decomposition in Appendix
A.1
, we find that the number of trivial singlets is equal to one, which corresponds to the term
T_{1}T_{2}
.

We also examine the example of flavons in the renormalizable scalar potential of Ref.
[
30
]
.
In that model two flavons
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are introduced, both transforming as
A_{4}
triplets.
This setup is directly analogous to the present case.
The only difference is that
\phi^{\prime}
changes sign under a
Z_{4}
transformation, so the full symmetry is
A_{4}\times Z_{4}
.
In the Hilbert series this is implemented by removing all terms that contain an odd power of
\phi^{\prime}
.
Taking the flavons to have mass dimension one, the Hilbert series for renormalizable operators is

HS=1+\phi^{2}+\phi^{\prime 2}+\phi^{3}+\phi\phi^{\prime 2}+2\phi^{4}+4\phi^{2}\phi^{\prime 2}+2\phi^{\prime 4}\,.

(3.33)

We find that there are
12
invariant operators in total, and the polynomial structure of each term matches exactly the
12
renormalizable structures given in Ref.
[
30
]
.

3.3
Computing Hilbert series with modular forms

When the Yukawa couplings are constructed from holomorphic modular forms in
A_{4}
modular symmetry, modular forms of arbitrary weight can be built from the fundamental modular form
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
through tensor products.
Since the tensor product of two modular forms of weights
k
and
\widetilde{k}
has weight
k+\widetilde{k}
, any higher-weight modular form can ultimately be expressed in terms of tensor products of
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
.
Therefore,
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
can be regarded as a spurion field and taken as the fundamental building block of the flavor structures.

For convenience, we use
Y
to denote
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
.
The plethystic exponential (PE) involving modular forms can be written as

\text{PE}[D,\phi,Y,Y^{*}]=\text{PE}[D,\phi]\cdot\text{PE}[Y]\cdot\text{PE}[Y^{*}]\,.

(3.34)

Here, the generating function for
Y
takes the form

\text{PE}[Y]=\exp\!\left(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{Y^{r}\,\chi(g^{r})}{r}\right).

(3.35)

Since modular forms are trivial singlets under both the Lorentz group and the gauge group, their characters depend only on the representation of the modular group.

With the
A_{4}
constraint in Eq. (
2.65
) imposed, the symmetric tensor product at weight
n
is constrained by removing the components proportional to
Y_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}^{(4)}
arising from the product of a weight-
(n-2)
symmetric tensor with
Y_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}^{(4)}
.
Taking this effect into account, we define the modified generating function

\text{PE}^{\prime}[Y]=\exp\!\left(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{Y^{r}\,\chi(g^{r})}{r}-\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\frac{Y^{2r}\,\chi_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(g^{r})}{r}\right),

(3.36)

where
\chi_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}
denotes the character of the
A_{4}
singlet representation
\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
.

For the model considered here, the generating function for the two modular forms
Y(\tau_{q})
and
Y(\tau_{e})
takes the form

\text{PE}^{\prime}[Y(\tau_{q}),Y(\tau_{e})]=\exp\!\left(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\left[\frac{Y^{r}(\tau_{q})\,\chi_{q}(g^{r})}{r}+\frac{Y^{r}(\tau_{e})\,\chi_{e}(g^{r})}{r}-\frac{Y^{2r}(\tau_{q})\,\chi_{q\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(g^{r})}{r}-\frac{Y^{2r}(\tau_{e})\,\chi_{e\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(g^{r})}{r}\right]\right).

(3.37)

Including the anti-holomorphic modular forms, the complete Hilbert series can be formulated as

\displaystyle H(D,\phi,Y,Y^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G_{d}|}\sum_{g\in G_{d}}\int_{G_{c}}d\mu_{G_{c}}\,\frac{1}{P(D,z)}\,\text{PE}^{\prime}\bigl[Y(\tau_{q}),Y(\tau_{e})\bigr]\,\text{PE}^{\prime}\bigl[Y^{*}(\tau_{q}),Y^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr]

(3.38)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i}\begin{cases}\text{PE}\bigl(\phi_{i};\chi(D,z,a,g)\bigr),&\text{if }\phi_{i}\text{ is boson},\\[2.0pt]
\text{PEF}\bigl(\phi_{i};\chi(D,z,a,g)\bigr),&\text{if }\phi_{i}\text{ is fermion},\end{cases}

with

G_{d}=A_{4q}\otimes A_{4e},\qquad G_{c}=G_{SO_{4}}\otimes G_{\rm gauge}\otimes U(1)_{q}\otimes U(1)_{qd}\otimes U(1)_{e}\otimes U(1)_{ed}.

(3.39)

Meanwhile, a more direct approach is to follow the leading-order Yukawa structure and construct holomorphic modular-invariant higher-dimensional operators in the schematic form

Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})}\,{Y_{\mathbf{r^{\prime}}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}\,\mathcal{O},

(3.40)

which is a combination of a holomorphic modular form, an anti-holomorphic modular form, and a normal field operator.
This can be expressed by the tensor-product relation

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}(D,\phi,Y,Y^{*})\right]_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=\bigl[(Y\otimes\dots\otimes Y)_{\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{Y}/2}}\otimes(Y^{*}\otimes\dots\otimes Y^{*})_{\mathrm{Sym}^{k_{Y}^{\prime}/2}}\otimes\mathcal{O}(D,\phi)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

(3.41)

\displaystyle\equiv\bigl[Y^{(k_{Y})}_{s}\otimes{Y^{*}_{s}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}\otimes\mathcal{O}(D,\phi)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

where
Y^{(k_{Y})}_{s}
and
{Y^{*}_{s}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}
denote the corresponding symmetric tensor products of
Y
and
Y^{*}
.

This relation implies separate weight counting and tensor-product constraints for holomorphic and anti-holomorphic factors,

k_{Y}=\sum_{i}k_{i},\quad k^{\prime}_{Y}=\sum_{j}k^{\prime}_{j},\quad\rho\otimes{\rho}^{*}\otimes\rho^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\rho^{(n)}\otimes{{\rho}^{(n+1)}}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes{{\rho}^{(n+m)}}^{*}\supset\mathbf{1},

(3.42)

for an operator containing
n
non-conjugated factors and
m
conjugated factors.
In addition, the equivalence between this tensor-product construction and the Hilbert-series counting can be verified explicitly.

Equivalently, one may treat all modular forms (e.g.
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})
,
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})
,
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})
,
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})
,
\dots
) as building blocks, with the restriction that each distinct modular form appears at most once in a given term.
The corresponding Hilbert series can then be written as

\displaystyle H\!\left(D,\phi,Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})},{Y_{\mathbf{r^{\prime}}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|G_{d}|}\sum_{g\in G_{d}}\int_{G_{c}}d\mu_{G_{c}}\,\frac{1}{P(D,z)}

(3.43)

\displaystyle\times\left(\prod_{k}\mathrm{PE}\!\left[Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})},{Y_{\mathbf{r^{\prime}}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}\right]\right)\prod_{i}\begin{cases}\mathrm{PE}\!\bigl(\phi_{i};\chi(D,z,a,g)\bigr),&\text{if }\phi_{i}\text{ is boson},\\
\mathrm{PEF}\!\bigl(\phi_{i};\chi(D,z,a,g)\bigr),&\text{if }\phi_{i}\text{ is fermion}.\end{cases}

Here,

\prod_{k}\text{PE}[Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})},{Y_{\mathbf{r^{\prime}}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}]=\prod_{k}\text{PE}[\{Y_{\mathbf{rq}}^{(k_{qY})}\}]\text{PE}[\{Y_{\mathbf{re}}^{(k_{eY})}\}]\text{PE}[\{{Y_{\mathbf{rq^{\prime}}}^{(k_{qY}^{\prime})}}^{*}\}]\text{PE}[\{{Y_{\mathbf{re^{\prime}}}^{(k_{eY}^{\prime})}}^{*}\}]\,,

(3.44)

with
\mathbf{rq}
,
\mathbf{rq^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{re}
,
\mathbf{re^{\prime}}
denoting representations of
A_{4q}
and
A_{4e}
, respectively.
Because each type of modular form appears at most once per term, the factor
\prod_{k}\text{PE}[\{Y^{(k_{qY})}_{\mathbf{rq}}(\tau_{q})\}]
reduces to a finite polynomial; schematically,

\prod_{k}\text{PE}[\{Y_{\mathbf{rq}}^{(k_{qY})}\}]\simeq\left(1+Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\chi_{q3}(g)+Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\chi_{q1}(g)+Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\chi_{q\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(g)+\dots\right).

(3.45)

By explicitly computing the Hilbert series in Eqs. (
3.38
) and (
3.43
), we find that each term obtained from (
3.43
) matches the corresponding contribution in Eq. (
3.38
).
For example, consider the dimension-six SMEFT operator
\mathcal{O}_{uu}
with flavor
u
and
\overline{u}
.
Equation (
3.38
) yields the term
7\,u^{2}{u^{\dagger}}^{2}{Y^{*}}^{4}Y^{4}
, while Eq. (
3.43
) resolves it into the following seven structures,

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

(3.46)

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle u^{2}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle u^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\;{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q}).

Here
a
and
b
label the two independent triplet modular forms of
A_{4}
at weight
8
.
Furthermore, both Hilbert series yield the same total number of operators:
2961
at mass dimension
6
and
360
at mass dimension
7
.

For (anti-)holomorphic modular forms, constructing operators via tensor products of modular forms is fully consistent with the alternative procedure of enumerating all modular forms at a given weight and then building operators.
The two approaches therefore provide a cross-check of each other.
To make the operator structures more transparent, we will base our discussion on the terms appearing in the Hilbert series in Eq. (
3.43
).

4
A_{4}
modular invariant SMEFT operators

In this section, we employ the Hilbert-series framework to systematically determine the operator flavor structures and their corresponding multiplicities.
The coefficients of the series directly give the number of independent operators associated with each structure.

As a first application, we analyze the dimension-five Weinberg operator and identify all flavor-invariant structures consistent with the
A_{4}^{(q)}\otimes A_{4}^{(e)}
modualr symmetry.
At dimension six, we proceed analogously and construct the explicit forms of all baryon- and lepton-number conserving operators.

In this way, we obtain a systematic classification of all relevant operators
considered in this work.

4.1
Dimension-5 case

At dimension five, the only operator is the Weinberg operator, whose explicit form is

\mathcal{O}_{5}=(LH)(LH)+\text{h.c.}\,.

(4.1)

A detailed discussion of modular-invariant SMEFT operators at this order can be found in Ref.
[
100
]
.
Before inserting the vacuum expectation value (VEV) of the Higgs field, there are three possible structures:

\displaystyle\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{3}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}}.

Using the tensor-product rules summarized in Appendix
A.1
, these three terms can be written explicitly as

\displaystyle\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{3}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}}={}

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{1}\big[2(L_{1}H)(L_{1}H)-(L_{2}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{2}H)\big]

(4.3)

\displaystyle+Y^{(4)}_{2}\big[2(L_{3}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{1}H)(L_{2}H)-(L_{2}H)(L_{1}H)\big]

\displaystyle+Y^{(4)}_{3}\big[2(L_{2}H)(L_{2}H)-(L_{1}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{1}H)\big]\,,

\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}}=Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\big[(L_{1}H)(L_{1}H)+(L_{2}H)(L_{3}H)+(L_{3}H)(L_{2}H)\big]\,,

(4.4)

\left[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\right]_{\mathbf{1}}=Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\big[(L_{2}H)(L_{2}H)+(L_{1}H)(L_{3}H)+(L_{3}H)(L_{1}H)\big]\,.

(4.5)

Here the weight-4 triplet modular form is written as

Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}=\begin{pmatrix}Y^{(4)}_{1}\\
Y^{(4)}_{2}\\
Y^{(4)}_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.6)

For the explicit tensor products of fields, the contractions into

[\cdots]_{\mathbf{1}}
,
[\cdots]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}
,
[\cdots]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}
,

[\cdots]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}
,
[\cdots]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}

are given by

[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{1}}=(L_{1}H)(L_{1}H)+(L_{2}H)(L_{3}H)+(L_{3}H)(L_{2}H)\,,

(4.7)

[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}=(L_{3}H)(L_{3}H)+(L_{1}H)(L_{2}H)+(L_{2}H)(L_{1}H)\,,

(4.8)

[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}=(L_{2}H)(L_{2}H)+(L_{1}H)(L_{3}H)+(L_{3}H)(L_{1}H)\,,

(4.9)

[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{3}}=[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}=\begin{pmatrix}2(L_{1}H)(L_{1}H)-(L_{2}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{2}H)\\
2(L_{3}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{1}H)(L_{2}H)-(L_{2}H)(L_{1}H)\\
2(L_{2}H)(L_{2}H)-(L_{1}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{1}H)\end{pmatrix}\!,

(4.10)

and

[(LH)(LH)]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}=\begin{pmatrix}(L_{2}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{2}H)\\
(L_{1}H)(L_{2}H)-(L_{2}H)(L_{1}H)\\
(L_{1}H)(L_{3}H)-(L_{3}H)(L_{1}H)\end{pmatrix}\!.

(4.11)

Within the Hilbert-series framework, and applying series (
3.43
), the dimension-five contribution can be written as

\text{Hilbert5}={Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}{l^{\dagger}}^{2}+{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}{l^{\dagger}}^{2}+{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}{l^{\dagger}}^{2}+Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,h^{2}l^{2}+Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,h^{2}l^{2}+Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,h^{2}l^{2}\,.

(4.12)

From this expression we see that the Weinberg operator and its conjugate together generate three distinct operator types and their conjugates.
As shown in Eq. (
4.2
), these provide the most elementary realization.
Moreover, each term in the Hilbert series can be put in one-to-one correspondence with the cases discussed in Ref.
[
100
]
.

In this case, the dimension-five Lagrangian can be written as

\mathcal{L}_{W}=\frac{1}{\Lambda}\Big(\big[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{3}}\big]_{\mathbf{1}}+g_{1}\big[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}}\big]_{\mathbf{1}}+g_{2}\big[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\big]_{\mathbf{1}}\Big)+\text{h.c.}\,.

(4.13)

An alternative modular-invariant construction of SMEFT operators is presented in
[
89
]
.
In that framework, all higher-dimensional operators are generated from three-point Yukawa interactions by integrating out heavy fields.
Using this construction, the Weinberg operator can be written as

(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}LH)_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\otimes(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}LH)_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,.

(4.14)

After contracting the Lorentz and
SU(2)_{L}
indices, the fully contracted operator is symmetric under
L_{i}\leftrightarrow L_{j}
.
So in this construction, there are five singlet contractions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}\otimes\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}})_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{5}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}\otimes\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathbf{1}}.

(4.19)

However, these contractions are not all independent, and they satisfy the linear relation

\mathcal{C}_{3}\;=\;4\big(\mathcal{C}_{1}-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big)\;-\;3\,\mathcal{C}_{4}\,.

(4.20)

Therefore, only four contractions are independent.

Another alternative construction is

\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{3}}\;\otimes\;\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{3}}\,.

(4.21)

Compared to Eq. (
4.14
), this is simply a different choice of basis.
The four singlet contractions in this basis can be chosen as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{1}}\;\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}\;\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}},

(4.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}\;\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}},

(4.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{4}

\displaystyle\equiv\Big(\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes\big[(LH)(LH)\big]_{\mathbf{3}}\Big)_{\mathbf{1}}.

(4.25)

This choice of basis removes the redundancy in Eq. (
4.20
).
Moreover, imposing the constraint in Eq. (
2.65
) yields

\big(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\big)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}=Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}=0\,,

(4.26)

which implies
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{3}=0
.
Hence, only three independent terms remain, in agreement with the result obtained from the Hilbert series.
Moreover, in the
holomorphic case they consider all pairwise tensor products between
modular forms of weights
k
and
k^{\prime}
, i.e. they work in the direct sum

\bigoplus_{k,k^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}}
. The natural
multiplication map

\mu_{k,k^{\prime}}:\;\mathcal{M}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}}

(4.27)

is generically not injective, and typically

\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k})\,\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}})>\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}})
. Consequently, many product tensors lie in

\ker\mu_{k,k^{\prime}}
and can be rewritten as linear combinations of a basis
of
\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}}
(i.e., modular forms of weight
k+k^{\prime}
). Keeping both the original products and those
linear combinations leads to an overcomplete set and thus introduces
redundancy.

4.2
Dimension-6 case

In the classification of dimension-6 SMEFT operators in Ref.
[
3
]
, we organize the operators according to the number of fermion fields they contain and present the corresponding results for each class.
All modular forms with weight
k>2
required in our analysis are listed in Appendix
B.2
.

•

Bilinear structures

1.

\overline{L}R+\text{h.c.}
:
There are three independent flavor structures of this type, which we denote by
(\overline{L}e)_{\mathbf{1}}
,
(\overline{Q}u)_{\mathbf{1}}
and
(\overline{Q}d)_{\mathbf{1}}
.
Here, “type” labels the contraction of left- and right-handed fermions into an
A_{4}
singlet.
The second column of Table
7
shows the schematic field content of each operator, while the third column gives the modular weight assigned to the corresponding modular form.
The subscripts “ed” and “qd” indicate that the modular form belongs to the lepton (
A_{4e}
) or quark (
A_{4q}
) sector, respectively.
The last column displays the generic
A_{4}
-invariant completion, written in terms of modular forms and the corresponding field contractions.
In the following, we show explicitly how the Hilbert series result for each flavor structure is matched to these
A_{4}
-invariant operators.

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

(\overline{L}e)_{\mathbf{1}}

(\overline{Q}u)_{\mathbf{1}}

6_{qd}

{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{q}}}^{*}\,[\overline{Q}_{L}\,\Gamma\,U_{R}]

(\overline{Q}d)_{\mathbf{1}}

\overline{Q}_{L}\,\Gamma\,D_{R}

2_{qd}

{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{q}}}^{*}\,[\overline{Q}_{L}\,\Gamma\,D_{R}]

Table 7:
Bilinear SMEFT operators of the form
\overline{L}R
,
together with their modular weights and schematic
A_{4}
-invariant
completions.

(a)

(\overline{\ell}e)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}
:
For this flavor structure, the Hilbert series yields the following combination of monomials in the lepton sector:

\displaystyle e\,h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+B\,e\,h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+e\,h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

(4.28)

\displaystyle+h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\mu\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+B\,h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\mu\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\mu\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

\displaystyle+h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\tau\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+B\,h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\tau\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+h\,{\ell^{\dagger}}\,\tau\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+\text{h.c.}\;.

Grouping these monomials by their gauge and Higgs content, one identifies
the three SMEFT operators
\mathcal{O}_{eH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{eW}
and

\mathcal{O}_{eB}
. Since these operators only involve leptons, they are trivial
singlets under
A_{4q}
by construction and must be promoted to invariants
of the full
A_{4e}\times A_{4q}
flavor symmetry by an appropriate
contraction in the
A_{4e}
sector.

In our setup
L_{L}
and
\overline{L}_{L}
transform as an
A_{4e}
triplet, while
e_{R}
,
\mu_{R}
and
\tau_{R}
are
A_{4e}
singlets.
Using the
A_{4}
tensor-product rules collected in Appendix
A.1
, we can combine the triplet modular form
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})
with
\overline{L}_{L}
into the three inequivalent
A_{4e}
singlets
\mathbf{1}
,
\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
:

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,\overline{L}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1}}\Gamma e_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,\overline{L}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\Gamma\mu_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

(4.30)

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,\overline{L}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\Gamma\tau_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;.

(4.31)

Each of these structures multiplies the operators
\mathcal{O}_{eH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{eW}
and
\mathcal{O}_{eB}
, thereby reproducing the three
A_{4e}
-invariant combinations counted by the Hilbert series.

(b)

(\overline{q}u)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}
:
For this type, the Hilbert series in the up-quark sector gives

\displaystyle B\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

(4.32)

\displaystyle+B\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,u\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+B\,c\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+c\,G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+c\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+B\,c\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+c\,G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+c\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+c\,h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+c\,h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+B\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+B\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+G\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,W\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+h\,{h^{\dagger}}^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,t\,{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+\mathrm{h.c.}\;.

These monomials can be organized into the SMEFT operators
\mathcal{O}_{uH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{uG}
,
\mathcal{O}_{uW}
and
\mathcal{O}_{uB}
.
Since they involve only quark fields, they are singlets under
A_{4e}
, and the non-trivial flavor structure resides in the
A_{4q}
sector.

Here,
Q_{L}
and
\overline{Q}_{L}
transform as
A_{4q}
triplets, while
u_{R}
,
c_{R}
and
t_{R}
are
A_{4q}
singlets.
The Hilbert series tells us that the modular forms entering this sector have weight
6
and transform as two independent triplets
Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})
and
Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})
.
Using the
A_{4}
tensor products, we can construct three
A_{4q}
singlets:

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a,\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1}}\Gamma u_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a,\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\Gamma c_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a,\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\Gamma t_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;.

Dressing
\mathcal{O}_{uH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{uG}
,
\mathcal{O}_{uW}
and
\mathcal{O}_{uB}
with these singlet combinations yields precisely the
A_{4q}
-invariant operators counted by the Hilbert series.

(c)

(\overline{q}d)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}
:
For the down-quark case, the Hilbert series result reads

\displaystyle d\,h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+B\,d\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+d\,G\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+d\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

(4.34)

\displaystyle+h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,s\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+B\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,s\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+G\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,s\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,s\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+b\,h^{2}\,{h^{\dagger}}\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+b\,B\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+b\,G\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+b\,h\,{q^{\dagger}}\,W\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+\text{h.c.}\;.

These monomials correspond to the SMEFT operators
\mathcal{O}_{dH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{dG}
,
\mathcal{O}_{dW}
and
\mathcal{O}_{dB}
.
As in the up-sector case, the operators are singlets under
A_{4e}
, and the non-trivial flavor structure is in the
A_{4q}
sector.

Using again that
Q_{L}
and
\overline{Q}_{L}
are
A_{4q}
triplets, while
d_{R}
,
s_{R}
and
b_{R}
are
A_{4q}
singlets, and that the relevant modular forms in this sector have weight
2
and transform as a triplet
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})
, we can construct the three
A_{4q}
singlet combinations

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1}}\Gamma d_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\Gamma s_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle\left({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,\overline{Q}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\Gamma b_{R}+\text{h.c.}\;.

Dressing
\mathcal{O}_{dH}
,
\mathcal{O}_{dG}
,
\mathcal{O}_{dW}
and
\mathcal{O}_{dB}
with these singlet structures gives the three
A_{4q}
-invariant operators, in one-to-one correspondence with the multiplicities obtained from the Hilbert series.

2.

\overline{L}L
:
There are two classes of operators of this type, distinguished by whether
the associated modular invariance resides in the lepton or quark sector:

–

Operators invariant under
A_{4e}
:

(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

(4.36)

–

Operator invariant under
A_{4q}
:

(\overline{q}\,q)_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.37)

type

weight

A_{4}

(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}

(a)

(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}
:
For this flavor structure the Hilbert series yields

14\,h\,h^{\dagger}\,\ell\,\ell^{\dagger}\,D\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,.

(4.38)

Thus there are 14 independent invariants with the schematic field content

\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}
dressed by the modular forms

Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})
.
The corresponding SMEFT operators are
\mathcal{O}_{Hl}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{O}_{Hl}^{(3)}
, so each of them is accompanied by 7 distinct

A_{4e}
-invariant flavor structures.

Using the
A_{4}
tensor products in Appendix
A.1
,
the product of two triplet modular forms decomposes as

Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\supset\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{s}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{a}
,
and similarly for the bilinear
\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}
.
The seven independent
A_{4e}
singlets can then be written as

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.39)

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

Here, the labels
\mathbf{3}_{s}
and
\mathbf{3}_{a}
denote, respectively,
the symmetric and antisymmetric triplet components in the tensor product

\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{3}
.

(b)

(\overline{q}q)_{\mathbf{1}}
:
The analysis in the quark sector proceeds in complete analogy with the
lepton case. The Hilbert series now gives

14\,h\,h^{\dagger}\,D\,q\,q^{\dagger}\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,,

(4.40)

corresponding to the operators
\mathcal{O}_{Hq}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{O}_{Hq}^{(3)}
. Since these operators involve only quark
fields, they are singlets under
A_{4e}
and must be completed to

A_{4q}
invariants.

Using that
Q_{L}
transforms as an
A_{4q}
triplet and following the same
tensor-product decomposition as above, we obtain the seven

A_{4q}
singlet structures:

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.41)

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\,\bigl(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

These seven structures for each of
\mathcal{O}_{Hq}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{O}_{Hq}^{(3)}
reproduce the total of 14

A_{4q}
-invariant operators predicted by the Hilbert series.

(4.48)

(\overline{d}d)_{\mathbf{1}}

d\,{d^{\dagger}}\,h\,{h^{\dagger}}\,D+h\,{h^{\dagger}}\,D\,s\,{s^{\dagger}}+b\,{b^{\dagger}}\,h\,{h^{\dagger}}\,D.

(4.50)

The corresponding SMEFT operator is
\mathcal{O}_{Hd}

(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}
:

\displaystyle\overline{d}_{R}\Gamma d_{R},

(4.51)

\displaystyle\overline{s_{R}}\Gamma s_{R},

\displaystyle\overline{b_{R}}\Gamma b_{R}.

4.

\overline{R}R^{\prime}+\text{h.c.}
:
In this class there is a single flavor structure, whose non-trivial modular
invariance resides in the quark sector:

–

Operator invariant under
A_{4q}
:

(\overline{u}d)_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.52)

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

(\overline{u}d)_{\mathbf{1}}

\overline{U}_{R}\,\Gamma\,D_{R}

4_{q}

Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}\,[\overline{U}_{R}\,\Gamma\,D_{R}]

For this flavor structure the Hilbert series gives

\displaystyle d\,h^{2}\,D\,{u^{\dagger}}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})+{c^{\dagger}}\,d\,h^{2}\,D\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})+{c^{\dagger}}\,h^{2}\,D\,s\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})

(4.53)

\displaystyle+h^{2}\,D\,s\,{t^{\dagger}}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})+b\,h^{2}\,D\,{t^{\dagger}}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})+b\,h^{2}\,D\,{t^{\dagger}}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})+\text{h.c.}\;.

This corresponds to 12 independent invariants built from the SMEFT operator

\mathcal{O}_{Hud}+\text{h.c.}
, with different flavor contractions in the
up- and down-type quark fields. In terms of the modular forms
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}

and
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}
, the
A_{4q}
-invariant combinations can be written as:

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{u}_{R}\Gamma d_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}\;,

(4.54)

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{c}_{R}\Gamma s_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{t}_{R}\Gamma b_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{c}_{R}\Gamma d_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{t}_{R}\Gamma s_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}+\text{h.c.}\;,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,\bigl(\overline{u}_{R}\Gamma b_{R}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}+\text{h.c.}\;.

In each case the product of the modular singlet representation
(
\mathbf{1}
or
\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
) with the corresponding bilinear
representation (
\mathbf{1}
or
\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
) gives an
A_{4q}
singlet,
so that all six structures contribute to
\mathcal{O}_{Hud}
in a way that
matches the Hilbert-series counting.

•

Quadrilinear Structure:

1.

(\overline{L}L)(\overline{L}L)
:
There are three classes of four-fermion operators of this type, which can be
classified according to their
A_{4}
transformation properties:

–

Operators invariant under
A_{4e}\otimes A_{4q}
:

\bigl[(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}\,(\overline{q}q)_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.55)

–

Operators invariant under
A_{4e}
:

\bigl[(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.56)

–

Operators invariant under
A_{4q}
:

\bigl[(\overline{q}q)(\overline{q}q)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.57)

(a)

\bigl[(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{q}q)_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}
:
For this type, the Hilbert series gives

98\,\ell\,\ell^{\dagger}\,q\,q^{\dagger}\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,.

(4.58)

The corresponding SMEFT operators are
\mathcal{O}_{lq}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{O}_{lq}^{(3)}
. Thus, for each of these operators there are

49
independent
A_{4}
-invariant flavor structures.

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{q}q)_{\mathbf{1}}

[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}]

2_{q}+2_{qd}+2_{e}+2_{ed}

\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}}{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{e}}}^{*}\bigr)\bigl({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}}{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{q}}}^{*}\bigr)[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L}]

The
A_{4}
-invariant completion factorizes into an
A_{4e}

an

\bigl[({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}}{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{e}}}^{*})(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,\bigl[({Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}_{q}}}{Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{q}}}^{*})(\overline{Q}_{L}\Gamma Q_{L})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

(4.59)

i.e. it is simply obtained by combining the bilinear structures of

(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)_{\mathbf{1}}
and
(\overline{q}q)_{\mathbf{1}}
.

(b)

\bigl[(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

For this type, the Hilbert series yields

\displaystyle 5\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+5\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

(4.60)

\displaystyle+10\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+5\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

\displaystyle+5\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+10\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

\displaystyle+10\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})+10\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})

\displaystyle+35\,\ell^{2}\,{\ell^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,.

The corresponding SMEFT operator is
\mathcal{O}_{ll}
.

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

\bigl[(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]

4_{e}+4_{ed}

\bigl({Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{e}}}^{*}\bigr)[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]

The
A_{4}
-invariant completion is obtained from

\Bigl[\bigl({Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{e}}}^{*}\bigr)[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]\Bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(4.61)

We now construct the
A_{4}
-invariant terms explicitly.

For the modular forms, there are six possible triplet combinations,
namely:

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}},

(4.62)

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}},

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e}),

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e}),

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e}),

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\,.

To obtain singlets, these triplets must be combined with triplets built
from
[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]
. There are
ten such triplet structures:

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}},

(4.63)

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}},

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}},

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}},

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}},

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{s}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}_{a}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}}.

In addition, for the modular forms there are seven independent singlet
combinations:

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.64)

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{e})\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}.

For each singlet irrep of
A_{4}
(trivial or non-trivial), there are five
possible contractions built from the factors

[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}][\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]
. We classify
them according to the resulting singlet representation:

–

\mathbf{1}
case:

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.65)

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}.

–

\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
case:

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

(4.66)

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}.

–

\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
case:

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

(4.67)

\displaystyle[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{s}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl([\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}]_{\mathbf{3}_{a}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}.

Summing up, we find

6\times 10\;(\text{triplet--triplet contractions})\;+\;7\times 5\;(\text{singlet--singlet contractions})\;=\;95

(4.68)

possible
A_{4e}
-invariant constructions. The coefficient of the
corresponding Hilbert-series term differs from that in
Ref.
[
89
]
.
The origin of this redundancy is the same as that discussed in Section
4.1
.

(c)

\bigl[(\overline{q}q)(\overline{q}q)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}
:
For this type, the Hilbert series gives

\displaystyle 10\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+10\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

(4.69)

\displaystyle+20\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+10\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+10\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+20\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+20\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})+20\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})

\displaystyle+70\,q^{2}\,{q^{\dagger}}^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\,.

The corresponding SMEFT operators are
\mathcal{O}_{qq}^{(1)}
and

2.

(\overline{R}R)(\overline{R}R)

The structure
(\overline{R}R)(\overline{R}R)

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{d}d)_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

\bigl[(\overline{e}e)(\overline{e}e)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

(4.72)

–

Operators invariant under
A_{4q}
:

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{u}u)(\overline{u}u)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(4.73)

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{d}d)(\overline{d}d)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{u}u)(\overline{d}d)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

(a)

\bigl[(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{u}u)_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}
:
The corresponding SMEFT operator is
\mathcal{O}_{eu}

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{u}u)_{\mathbf{1}}

4_{q}+4_{qd}

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}^{(4)}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}_{q}}}^{*}\bigr)[\overline{E}_{R}\Gamma E_{R}][\overline{U}_{R}\Gamma U_{R}]

We directly give the construction types. As for the bilinear structures

(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}
and
(\overline{u}u)_{\mathbf{1}}
discussed
above, in the
(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}

. The

3\;(\text{num.\ of }(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}})\times 21\;(\text{num.\ of }(\overline{u}u)_{\mathbf{1}})=63\,.

(4.74)

(b)

\bigl[(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{d}d)_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

The corresponding SMEFT operator is
\mathcal{O}_{ed}

type

weight

A_{4}
invariant

(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{d}d)_{\mathbf{1}}

0

[\overline{E}_{R}\Gamma E_{R}]_{\mathbf{1}}[\overline{D}_{R}\Gamma D_{R}]_{\mathbf{1}}

This case is simply the product of the bilinear sectors

(\overline{e}e)_{\mathbf{1}}
and
(\overline{d}d)_{\mathbf{1}}

(4.75)

type

weight

The construction can be separated into two parts: one involving the modular

Modular forms:

The possible contractions of modular forms at weight

are classified

A_{4}

–

Tensor products of
A_{4}
modular forms (singlet

\mathbf{1}
):

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

(4.76)

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

Tensor products of
A_{4}
modular forms (non-trivial singlet

\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
):

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

(4.77)

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}}^{*}(\tau_{q})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}.

A_{4}

\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
):

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

(4.78)

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{q})\,{Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}(\tau_{q}),
