\displaystyle+\mu^{2}\mu^{\dagger}\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+\mu^{2}\mu^{\dagger}\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}+\mu^{2}\mu^{\dagger}\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}

\displaystyle+\mu\tau(\tau^{\dagger})^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+\mu\tau(\tau^{\dagger})^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}+\mu\tau(\tau^{\dagger})^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}

\displaystyle+\mu^{\dagger}\tau^{2}\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}+\mu^{\dagger}\tau^{2}\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}

\displaystyle+(\mu^{\dagger})^{2}\tau^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+(\mu^{\dagger})^{2}\tau^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}+(\mu^{\dagger})^{2}\tau^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}

\displaystyle+\mu^{2}(\tau^{\dagger})^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}+\mu^{2}(\tau^{\dagger})^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}

\displaystyle+\mu\mu^{\dagger}\tau\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+\mu\mu^{\dagger}\tau\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,.

The corresponding invariant structures are

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

(5.50)

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}{Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,\bigl[(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\tau_{R})_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

–

Operators involving
(\overline{L}L)(\overline{R}R)
:

\bigl[(\overline{l}l)(\overline{e}e)\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(5.51)

We can separate these operators into nine cases, according to the flavor
assignments of the right-handed fields:

*

e=1
,
e^{\dagger}=1
:

The Hilbert series gives

e\,e^{\dagger}\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\,Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}+2\,e\,e^{\dagger}\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}+2\,e\,e^{\dagger}\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\,Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}+7\,e\,e^{\dagger}\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\,.

(5.52)

The corresponding
A_{4}
-invariant combinations can be written as

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(5.53)

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}},

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{e}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}.

*

e=1
,
\mu^{\dagger}=1
:

The Hilbert series gives

e\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+2\,e\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,.

(5.54)

The corresponding invariant operators are

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(5.55)

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}.

*

e=1
,
\tau^{\dagger}=1
:

The Hilbert series gives

e\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\tau^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}+2\,e\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\tau^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,.

(5.56)

The invariant combinations may be written as

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{1}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(5.57)

\displaystyle\bigl[{Y^{(-2)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}}

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma e_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}.

*

\mu=1
,
e^{\dagger}=1
:

This case is simply the complex conjugate of the case

e=1
,
\mu^{\dagger}=1
.

*

\mu=1
,
\mu^{\dagger}=1
:

The Hilbert series gives

l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\mu^{\dagger}\,D+2\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\mu^{\dagger}\,Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}+2\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\mu^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,.

(5.58)

The corresponding invariants can be summarized as

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(5.59)

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\mu}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,{Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}.

*

\mu=1
,
\tau^{\dagger}=1
:

The Hilbert series gives

l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\tau^{\dagger}+2\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\tau^{\dagger}\,Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}+2\,l\,l^{\dagger}\,\mu\,\tau^{\dagger}\,{Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}\,.

(5.60)

The corresponding invariants are

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(5.61)

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle\bigl[(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}(\overline{\tau}_{R}\Gamma\mu_{R})\bigr]_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathbf{3}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,{Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}}^{*}.

*

\tau=1
,
e^{\dagger}=1
:

This case is the complex conjugate of the case

e=1
,
\tau^{\dagger}=1
.

*

\tau=1
,
\mu^{\dagger}=1
:

This case is the complex conjugate of the case

\mu=1
,
\tau^{\dagger}=1
.

*

\tau=1
,
\tau^{\dagger}=1
:

The construction rules are identical to those in the case

\mu=1
,
\mu^{\dagger}=1
, with the flavor index
replacement
\mu\rightarrow\tau
.

6
Conclusion

In our work, we first reviewed the Standard Model flavor structure, including the origin of fermion mass matrices and the CKM/PMNS mixings. As one possible solution to the flavor problem, we recalled the MFV implementation in SMEFT, in which the SM Yukawa matrices
Y_{u}
,
Y_{d}
, and
Y_{e}
provide the only sources of flavor and CP violation in all higher-dimensional operators. In parallel, we also reviewed the traditional discrete flavor-symmetry framework, and introduced modular flavor symmetry as an economical realization of flavor breaking, where Yukawa couplings are furnished by modular forms rather than flavon alignments. Building on this, we followed the constructions in Ref.
[
89
]
and constructed a modular-symmetric Standard Model by taking the full modular flavor group to be
A_{4}^{(q)}\otimes A_{4}^{(e)}
, assigning left-handed fermions to
A_{4}
triplets and right-handed fermions to inequivalent singlets, and writing renormalizable Yukawa interactions.
The resulting holomorphic Yukawa couplings admit an interpretation as the low-energy imprint of a modular-invariant
\mathcal{N}=1
SUSY UV completion, where they descend from holomorphic superpotential interactions.
In the holomorphic case, we adopted an MFV-like assumption that the leading-order Yukawa sector constitutes the only source of flavor breaking relevant for constructing higher-dimensional operators.
We restricted the operator construction to a finite set of holomorphic modular-form multiplets and did not treat
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
as an independent building block. Instead, we took the simplest form of the kinetic terms and allowed
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
to appear there, as required by modular invariance, while excluding any
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
-dependent structures from the higher-dimensional operator basis. This prescription yielded a finite operator tower, even though
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
can enter the kinetic sector.
This modular-symmetric SM setup provides the reference framework for constructing modular-invariant SMEFT operators using the Hilbert-series method.

In our setup, the breaking of
A_{4}^{(q)}\otimes A_{4}^{(e)}
is entirely controlled by the vacuum expectation values
\langle\tau_{q}\rangle
and
\langle\tau_{e}\rangle
. Their effects in higher-dimensional operators are encoded solely through the corresponding modular forms. Accordingly, we took the modular forms appearing in the renormalizable Yukawa sector as the fundamental spurions of the EFT, and built all higher-dimensional operator structures from them. In the holomorphic
N=3
case, all higher-weight modular forms are generated from the fundamental weight-
2
triplet
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
via symmetric tensor products, so that
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
serves as the unique modular-form spurion. Using this property, we constructed the modular Hilbert series and verified that the resulting operator basis was organized in close analogy with the Yukawa sector, which significantly simplified the classification.
The resulting modular-invariant SMEFT contained six independent dimension-
5
operators,
2961
dimension-
6
operators, and
360
dimension-
7
operators. The explicit dimension-
5
operators were summarized in Table
3
, while the dimension-
6
results were collected in Tables
4
and
5
. We constructed all dimension-
5
operators and all baryon- and lepton-number conserving dimension-
6
operators. In Appendix
C.3
, we further discussed the structure of bilinear dimension-
7
operators without field-strength tensors.

We then extended the construction to non-holomorphic modular forms. Unlike the minimal assumption adopted in the holomorphic case, we imposed a constructive assumption in the non-holomorphic setting to avoid an infinite proliferation of operators. For higher-dimensional operators, we assumed that the required automorphy-factor compensation was provided solely by a single modular form, or together with a complex conjugate form if necessary.
This is because non-holomorphic modular forms were not closed under multiplication. Under this assumption, higher-dimensional operators could still be organized in the familiar tensor-product form,

\bigl[Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})}\,{Y_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}\,\mathcal{O}\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}
,
as in the holomorphic case. The assumption was also consistent with the fact that, at sufficiently large weight, in particular for weights
k\geq 4
in the
N=3
case, the relevant polyharmonic Maa
\upbeta
forms coincided with ordinary holomorphic modular forms. Within the benchmark Standard Model setup with Maaß forms, this reproduced the Weinberg-operator structure of Ref.
[
90
]
and allowed us to derive the complete set of dimension-
6
operators in the same non-holomorphic modular setting.

In the holomorphic part of this work we performed a detailed comparison with the modular-invariant SMEFT operator construction of Ref.
[
89
]
. Our Hilbert-series method automatically accounted for the algebraic relations among modular forms, typically in the Weinberg operator and for four-fermion current–current operators

(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})(\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L})
, and thus yielded an independent and complete operator basis. A concrete illustration of the resulting redundancies was given in Sec.
4.1
, where we used the Weinberg operator as an explicit example.
After removing redundant operators, the corresponding Hilbert-series expressions are given in
Eq. (
4.12
) for the Weinberg operator,
Eq. (
4.60
) for
(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)(\overline{\ell}\,\ell)
,
and Eq. (
4.69
) for
(\overline{q}\,q)(\overline{q}\,q)
.
The other operators, for all bilinear operators discussed there, as well as for the four-fermion operators built purely from right-handed fields and their conjugates, the total multiplicities agreed with our results. The apparent difference concerned how modular forms were embedded into the SMEFT operators.
Moreover, our Hilbert series allows us to extract the operator structures explicitly in the form

\bigl[\,Y_{\mathbf{r}}^{(k_{Y})}\,{Y_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}^{(k_{Y}^{\prime})}}^{*}\,\mathcal{O}\,\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

without having to introduce couplings that involve multiple insertions of
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
and its complex conjugate.

In the SMEFT we can classify operators as holomorphic or non-holomorphic by their chiral Lorentz structure.
Holomorphic operators are built from the components
\{\,X^{-}_{\mu\nu},\,\bar{R},\,L\,\}

[
102
]
, where the complex (anti-)self-dual decomposition of field strengths is defined by

X_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{\mu\nu}\mp i\,\widetilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\right)\,,\qquad\widetilde{X}_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}=\pm i\,X_{\mu\nu}^{\pm}\,,

(6.1)

with

\widetilde{X}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,X^{\rho\sigma}\,,\qquad X_{\mu\nu}\in\{G_{\mu\nu}^{A},\,W_{\mu\nu}^{I},\,B_{\mu\nu}\},

(6.2)

and scalar fields such as the Higgs do not enter the holomorphy classification.
With this criterion, the unique dimension-five operator is the holomorphic Weinberg operator
(LH)(LH)
.
At dimension six, the fermionic operators can be organized schematically as
5
5

5

Ref.
[
102
]
classifies the Yukawa-like class
\psi^{2}H^{3}
as non-holomorphic, since these operators can be removed in favor of non-holomorphic structures using the EOM. In contrast, we focus on the chirality-based operator structure and therefore treat
\psi^{2}H^{3}
as holomorphic in our classification.

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{h}\subset\Bigl\{

\displaystyle\psi^{2}XH,\ \psi^{2}H^{3},\ (\overline{L}R)(\overline{L}R),\ \mathcal{O}_{qqq},\ \mathcal{O}_{duu}\Bigr\},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}\subset\Bigl\{

\displaystyle\psi^{2}H^{2}D,\ (\overline{L}L)(\overline{L}L),\ (\overline{R}R)(\overline{R}R),\ (\overline{L}L)(\overline{R}R),\ (\overline{L}R)(\overline{R}L)+\text{h.c.},\ \mathcal{O}_{duq},\ \mathcal{O}_{qqu}\Bigr\},

(6.4)

where we omit the bosonic classes in this summary.

In our assumption that the SM Yukawa couplings are furnished by holomorphic modular forms, together with an MFV-like organizing principle, modular-invariant SMEFT operators are constructed by dressing an underlying SMEFT operator structure with appropriate (anti-)holomorphic modular-form factors.
Accordingly, whether an operator is completed with purely holomorphic modular-form factors or with non-holomorphic mixed factors built from holomorphic modular forms and their complex conjugates is governed primarily by whether the underlying SMEFT structure is holomorphic or non-holomorphic in the above chirality-based sense.
Within this MFV-like setup, the matching of modular-invariant SMEFT operators can be interpreted consistently in terms of a modular-invariant SUSY UV completion, subject to two minimal restrictions on admissible matching channels:

1.

Minimal
\tau
-dependence.

The modulus
\tau
enters the EFT only through (anti-)holomorphic modular forms.

2.

\tau
-independent heavy masses.

Heavy-mediator mass parameters are taken to be
\tau
-independent constants.

These conditions confine
\tau
-dependence to interaction couplings through holomorphic modular forms (and, when required, their complex conjugates), and exclude modulus-dependent propagator denominators such as
1/M(\tau)^{2}
.
They also motivate discarding kinetic-mediated contributions whenever modular covariance forces explicit
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
-dependent prefactors to appear in covariant kinetic structures, since such prefactors would propagate into Wilson coefficients and lie outside our minimal operator class. For holomorphic operators, the required holomorphic modular forms typically originate only from F-term structures in a SUSY UV completion, whereas D-term structures in the SUSY UV generally introduce non-holomorphic modular factors, preventing the matched EFT operator from remaining holomorphic.

A representative non-holomorphic modular-invariant operator is

\Bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}(\tau)\,Y^{(4)\,*}_{\mathbf{r}_{e}}(\tau)\,\bigl[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr]\,\bigl[\overline{L}_{L}\Gamma L_{L}\bigr]\Bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(6.5)

One SUSY-compatible UV realization is a Zee-type holomorphic trilinear coupling involving a heavy scalar mediator
S
that is a modular singlet,

\bigl[Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,(L^{T}Ci\sigma_{2}L)_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\,S\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}},

(6.6)

so that integrating out
S
generates the above four-fermion structure at low energies.
Alternatively, a current–current structure can be produced by integrating out a heavy vector state
X_{\mu}
coupled to lepton currents, schematically from an interaction of the form

\Bigl[(Y^{(2)}Y^{(2)\,*})_{\mathbf{r}}\,\bigl(\overline{L}_{L}\gamma^{\mu}L_{L}\,X_{\mu}\bigr)_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\Bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}.

(6.7)

While the stringy factorization ansatz of Ref.
[
89
]
naturally accommodates superpotential-type trilinear couplings, and hence holomorphic modular-form dependence in the underlying Yukawa interactions.
However, it does not fix a generic light–light–heavy current coupling of the form
\overline{L}_{L}\gamma^{\mu}L_{L}X_{\mu}
.
Therefore, the operator construction in their paper cannot be obtained from the D-term sector of a modular SUSY UV completion.
In our MFV-like setup, any such current coupling is required not to introduce new independent flavor spurions; its flavor tensor must be expandable in modular-form combinations.

In summary, our MFV-like assumption can be motivated not only from the F-term sector of a modular SUSY UV completion, but also provides a general restriction on admissible modular SUSY D-term structures. This restriction avoids the appearance of infinite kinetic terms of the kind discussed in Ref.
[
42
]
.
Within the modular-invariant SMEFT, holomorphic operators are typically generated only from F-term structures in modular SUSY UV completions in tree-level matching, whereas non-holomorphic operators can generally arise from either F-term or D-term structures. A more detailed discussion is provided in Appendix
C.2
.

Finally, this MFV-like assumption still suffers from several shortcomings. First, it relies on simplifying assumptions neglecting the kinetic term of
\tau
and omitting additional contributions involving
\mathrm{Im}\,\tau
together with modular forms. Second, the resulting operator count is not dramatically smaller than in the conventional SM case, so many free parameters remain in phenomenological applications. For comparison, at dimension six the number of invariant operators with three generations is
3045
, whereas our result is only slightly smaller. Moreover, the number of baryon-number–conserving operators in our setup is
2733
, which is still more than the
894
baryon-number–conserving operators in the dimension-6 MFV-SMEFT. In this sense, the modular constraints considered here are not yet sufficiently restrictive. Addressing this issue requires additional constraints within the present framework.

Appendix A
Flavor construction in general
A_{4}
flavor symmetry

A.1
Tensor product rule of
A_{4}
group

In the discrete flavor group
A_{4}
, the tensor products of irreducible representations decompose as

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\;=\;\mathbf{1},\qquad\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\;=\;\mathbf{1},

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\;=\;\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}},\qquad\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\;=\;\mathbf{1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{3}\;=\;\mathbf{3},\quad\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{3}\;=\;\mathbf{3},\quad\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{3}\;=\;\mathbf{3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{3}\;=\;\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}\,.

Here
\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}
and
\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}
denote, respectively, the symmetric and antisymmetric components in

\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{3}
.

Specifically, the group
A_{4}
admits the presentation

S^{2}=(ST)^{3}=T^{3}=e,

(A.2)

and it can be realized as a semidirect product of the residual subgroups

G_{\ell}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{3}=\{e,T,T^{2}\},\qquad G_{\nu}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{e,S,TST^{2},T^{2}ST\},\quad\text{so that}\quad A_{4}\simeq G_{\nu}\rtimes G_{\ell}.

(A.3)

More concretely, we label the four vertices of a regular tetrahedron by
1,2,3,4
.
The generator
S
is the permutation
(12)(34)
, corresponding to
a
180^{\circ}
rotation about the axis through the midpoints of the opposite
edges connecting vertices
1
–
2
and
3
–
4
. The generator
T
is the
permutation
(123)
, corresponding to a
120^{\circ}
rotation about the axis
through vertex
4
and the center of the opposite face
(123)
.
Using the generators
S
and
T
and the group relations in Eq. (
A.2
),
the group
A_{4}
has 12 elements, which can be chosen as

\{e,\,S,\,TST^{2},\,T^{2}ST,\,T,\,TS,\,ST,\,STS,\,T^{2},\,ST^{2},\,T^{2}S,\,TST\}.

These 12 elements fall into four conjugacy classes,

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=\{e\},\quad C_{2}=\{S,\,TST^{2},\,T^{2}ST\},

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=\{T,\,TS,\,ST,\,STS\},\quad C_{4}=\{T^{2},\,ST^{2},\,T^{2}S,\,TST\}.

Since
A_{4}
has four conjugacy classes, it has four inequivalent irreducible representations.
By the standard sum–of–squares formula for the dimensions
d_{i}
of the irreducible representations,

\sum_{i=1}^{4}d_{i}^{2}=|A_{4}|=12,

with
d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. The only possibility is

(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4})=(1,1,1,3),

so
A_{4}
has three one-dimensional representations and one three-dimensional representation.
Using the character orthogonality relations, we obtain the character table shown in Table
2
.

In the three–dimensional irreducible representation of
A_{4}
, the
generators
S
and
T
can be written in two convenient bases.

(i) Eigenbasis of
S
.

With eigenvalues
1,-1,-1
for
S
, the generators are represented by

T=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad S=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(A.4)

(ii) Eigenbasis of
T
.

Let
\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}
. In the basis where

T^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\omega,\omega^{2})
, the generators read

T^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\omega&0\\
0&0&\omega^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad S^{\prime}=\frac{1}{3}\begin{pmatrix}-1&2&2\\
2&-1&2\\
2&2&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(A.5)

The two bases are related by the unitary matrix

P=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
1&\omega&\omega^{2}\\
1&\omega^{2}&\omega\end{pmatrix},

such that

T^{\prime}=P^{-1}TP,\qquad S^{\prime}=P^{-1}SP.

In the eigenbasis of
S
, denote two
A_{4}
triplets by

a^{T}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
and
b^{T}=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})
, with

\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}
. In this basis, the tensor-product decomposition

\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{3}=\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}\oplus\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\;(ab)\;=\;a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}+a_{3}b_{3},

(A.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;(ab)^{\prime}\;=\;a_{1}b_{1}+\omega^{2}a_{2}b_{2}+\omega\,a_{3}b_{3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;(ab)^{\prime\prime}\;=\;a_{1}b_{1}+\omega\,a_{2}b_{2}+\omega^{2}a_{3}b_{3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}

\displaystyle\equiv\;(ab)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}a_{3}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{3}\\[2.0pt]
a_{1}b_{3}+a_{3}b_{1}\\[2.0pt]
a_{2}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}\;\equiv\;(ab)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}a_{3}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{3}\\[2.0pt]
a_{1}b_{3}-a_{3}b_{1}\\[2.0pt]
a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

In the eigenbasis of
S
, the singlet contractions of two
triplets can be written in matrix form as

(ab)_{\mathbf{1}}\equiv a^{T}G_{1}\,b,\qquad(ab)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}\equiv a^{T}G_{1^{\prime}}\,b,\qquad(ab)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}\equiv a^{T}G_{1^{\prime\prime}}\,b.

(A.7)

Here the coefficient matrices are

G_{1}=\mathrm{diag}(1,1,1),\qquad G_{1^{\prime}}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\omega^{2},\omega),\qquad G_{1^{\prime\prime}}=\mathrm{diag}(1,\omega,\omega^{2}),

(A.8)

with
\omega\equiv e^{2\pi i/3}
.
The
S
- and
T
-eigenbases are related by the change of basis

a^{\prime}_{i}=P^{-1}_{ij}a_{j},\qquad b^{\prime}_{i}=P^{-1}_{ij}b_{j}.

(A.9)

Using this transformation, the singlet contractions can be recast in the
eigenbasis of
T
. In the following,
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
and
(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})

denote the components in the
T
-eigenbasis:

(ab)_{\mathbf{1}}\;=\;a_{1}b_{1}+a_{3}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{3},

(A.10)

(ab)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}\;=\;a_{3}b_{3}+a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1},

(A.11)

(ab)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}\;=\;a_{2}b_{2}+a_{3}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{3}.

(A.12)

For the triplet products, we first express the contractions in the
S
-basis
and then apply the change of basis above to obtain their forms in the

T
-basis. The symmetric and antisymmetric triplets are

(ab)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}}=\begin{pmatrix}2a_{1}b_{1}-a_{2}b_{3}-a_{3}b_{2}\\[2.0pt]
2a_{3}b_{3}-a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1}\\[2.0pt]
2a_{2}b_{2}-a_{3}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{3}\end{pmatrix},

(A.13)

(ab)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{3}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{3}\\[2.0pt]
a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2}\\[2.0pt]
a_{1}b_{3}-a_{3}b_{1}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.14)

These five tensor-product rules are the ones adopted in this work.

A.2
Flavor construction in a generic
A_{4}
flavor symmetry

For a given fermion species (e.g. the up-type quarks), one may perform a flavor rotation on the flavor triplet
\Psi_{u}=(u,c,t)^{T}
as

\Psi_{u}\;\to\;U\,\Psi_{u},\qquad U\in U(3),

(A.15)

without changing
\mathcal{L}_{\rm kinetic}+\mathcal{L}_{\rm gauge}
. All such related
field triplets define different weak bases (or flavor bases). Once Yukawa
couplings are switched on, the up-type mass matrix
M_{u}
is in general non-diagonal in a
generic weak basis and is diagonalized by a biunitary transformation,

U_{L}^{u\dagger}M_{u}U_{R}^{u}=M_{u}^{\rm diag}\equiv\mathrm{diag}(m_{u},m_{c},m_{t}).

(A.16)

This transformation maps the weak basis to the mass basis.
When a non-Abelian discrete flavor symmetry is imposed, the weak-basis freedom is
restricted by the representation assignments. As a concrete example, consider an

A_{4}
flavor symmetry where the three up-type quark flavors are assigned to the
one-dimensional irreducible representations

u_{R}\sim\mathbf{1},\qquad c_{R}\sim\mathbf{1}^{\prime},\qquad t_{R}\sim\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime},

(A.17)

and assemble them into a reducible flavor triplet

\Psi_{u}=(u,\,c,\,t)^{T}
. In the standard
A_{4}
presentation with generators
S
and
T

satisfying
S^{2}=(ST)^{3}=T^{3}=e
, the action of
T
on these one-dimensional irreps is

\mathbf{1}:~T=1,\qquad\mathbf{1}^{\prime}:~T=\omega,\qquad\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}:~T=\omega^{2},\qquad\omega=e^{2\pi i/3},

(A.18)

so that in the triplet space one has the diagonal representation matrix

\rho_{u}(T)=\mathrm{diag}(1,\omega,\omega^{2}).

(A.19)

A weak basis where
\rho_{u}(T)
takes the diagonal form in Eq. (
A.19
) is referred to
as the
T
-diagonal basis; choosing the assignments in Eq. (
A.17
) is therefore
equivalent to fixing a
T
-diagonal weak basis.

Under a general weak-basis change in Eq. (
A.15
), the representation matrix of

T
transforms by similarity,

\rho_{u}(T)\;\to\;\rho_{u}^{\prime}(T)=U\,\rho_{u}(T)\,U^{\dagger}.

(A.20)

Requiring that the irrep assignments in Eq. (
A.17
) remain valid in the transformed
basis is equivalent to demanding
\rho_{u}^{\prime}(T)=\rho_{u}(T)
, i.e.

U\,\rho_{u}(T)=\rho_{u}(T)\,U
. Writing this condition in components gives, for

i\neq j
,

\bigl(\rho_{u}(T)_{jj}-\rho_{u}(T)_{ii}\bigr)\,U_{ij}=0.

(A.21)

Since the eigenvalues of
\rho_{u}(T)
are
\{1,\omega,\omega^{2}\}
and are all distinct, we have

U_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
. Hence the only allowed weak-basis transformations that
preserve the assignments in Eq. (
A.17
) are rephasings,

U=\mathrm{diag}(e^{i\alpha_{1}},e^{i\alpha_{2}},e^{i\alpha_{3}}),

(A.22)

so the original
U(3)
freedom collapses to independent phase redefinitions.

In a generic
A_{4}
flavor framework with the lepton mixing matrix approximated by the tri-bimaximal (TB) form in Eq. (
1.1
), the lepton-sector Yukawa interactions can be implemented by introducing
A_{4}
-triplet flavons
\varphi_{T}
and
\varphi_{S}
(both transforming as
\mathbf{3}
of
A_{4}
) and an
A_{4}
-trivial singlet
\xi
. Schematically, one may write
[
30
,
40
]

\mathcal{L}=y_{e}\,e^{c}\,(\varphi_{T}\,L)\,\frac{h_{d}}{\Lambda}+y_{\mu}\,\mu^{c}\,(\varphi_{T}\,L)^{\prime}\,\frac{h_{d}}{\Lambda}+y_{\tau}\,\tau^{c}\,(\varphi_{T}\,L)^{\prime\prime}\,\frac{h_{d}}{\Lambda}+\frac{x_{a}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,\xi\,(Lh_{u}\,Lh_{u})+\frac{x_{b}}{\Lambda^{2}}\,(\varphi_{S}\,Lh_{u}\,Lh_{u})+\text{h.c.}+\ldots,

(A.23)

where
\Lambda
is the cutoff scale, and the leptonic and scalar fields transform under
A_{4}
as

\{e^{c},h_{u},h_{d}\}\sim\mathbf{1}
,
\mu^{c}\sim\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}
,
\tau^{c}\sim\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
, and
L\sim\mathbf{3}
.
The symbols
(\cdots)_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1^{\prime}},\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}
denote
A_{4}
contractions into the corresponding singlet representations.
Specifically, we adopt the eigenbasis of the
A_{4}
generator
S
for all triplet fields. In this basis, the flavon components
\varphi_{Ti}
and
\varphi_{Si}
, as well as the singlet
\xi
, are taken to be
real
scalar fields.

For the one-dimensional irreducible representations,
each field
e^{c}
,
h_{u}
,
h_{d}
,
\mu^{c}
, and
\tau^{c}

is an ordinary complex scalar or spin-
\tfrac{1}{2}
field (as appropriate) that additionally transforms according to its assigned
A_{4}
irrep.

The left-handed
SU(2)_{L}
lepton doublets are

L_{\ell}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{L}\\
\nu_{\ell}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\ell=e,\mu,\tau,

(A.24)

and are assembled into an
A_{4}
triplet in flavor space,

L\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}L_{e}\\
L_{\mu}\\
L_{\tau}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathbf{3},

(A.25)

so that the three flavors
(L_{e},L_{\mu},L_{\tau})
transform into one another under the triplet representation of
A_{4}
.
With these field representations, once the Higgs fields develop their VEVs and the flavons acquire vacuum expectation values, the electroweak gauge symmetry

SU(2)_{L}\times U(1)_{Y}
and the
A_{4}
flavor symmetry are spontaneously broken.
We assume the following vacuum alignment for the flavons,

\langle\varphi_{T}\rangle=(v_{T},\,0,\,0),\qquad\langle\varphi_{S}\rangle=(v_{S},\,v_{S},\,v_{S}),\qquad\langle\xi\rangle=u,

(A.26)

and the electroweak Higgs doublets acquire VEVs

\langle h_{u}\rangle=v_{u},\qquad\langle h_{d}\rangle=v_{d}.

(A.27)

After symmetry breaking, the Yukawa terms take the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=v_{d}\,\frac{v_{T}}{\Lambda}\!\left(y_{e}\,e^{c}e\;+\;y_{\mu}\,\mu^{c}\mu\;+\;y_{\tau}\,\tau^{c}\tau\right)

(A.28)

\displaystyle+\,x_{a}\,v_{u}^{2}\,\frac{u}{\Lambda^{2}}\!\left(\nu_{e}\nu_{e}\;+\;2\,\nu_{\mu}\nu_{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle+\,x_{b}\,v_{u}^{2}\,\frac{2v_{S}}{3\Lambda^{2}}\!\left(\nu_{e}\nu_{e}\;+\;\nu_{\mu}\nu_{\mu}\;+\;\nu_{\tau}\nu_{\tau}\;-\;\nu_{e}\nu_{\mu}\;-\;\nu_{\mu}\nu_{\tau}\;-\;\nu_{\tau}\nu_{e}\right)\;+\;\text{h.c.}\;+\;\cdots\,.

In this setup, the charged-lepton and neutrino mass matrices take the form:

m_{\ell}\;=\;v_{d}\,\frac{v_{T}}{\Lambda}\!\begin{pmatrix}y_{e}&0&0\\[2.0pt]
0&y_{\mu}&0\\[2.0pt]
0&0&y_{\tau}\end{pmatrix},\qquad m_{\nu}\;=\;\frac{v_{u}^{2}}{\Lambda}\!\begin{pmatrix}a+\tfrac{2}{3}b&-\tfrac{1}{3}b&-\tfrac{1}{3}b\\[4.0pt]
-\tfrac{1}{3}b&\tfrac{2}{3}b&a-\tfrac{1}{3}b\\[4.0pt]
-\tfrac{1}{3}b&a-\tfrac{1}{3}b&\tfrac{2}{3}b\end{pmatrix},

(A.29)

with

a\equiv 2x_{a}\,\frac{u}{\Lambda},\qquad b\equiv 2x_{b}\,\frac{v_{S}}{\Lambda}\,.

(A.30)

In this setup the neutrino mass matrix is diagonalized by the tri-bimaximal matrix

U_{\mathrm{TB}}
in Eq. (
1.1
), namely

U_{\mathrm{TB}}^{T}\,m_{\nu}\,U_{\mathrm{TB}}\;=\;\frac{v_{u}^{2}}{\Lambda}\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(m_{1}=a+b,\;m_{2}=a,\;m_{3}=-a+b\bigr)\,.

(A.31)

In particular, when the flavon acquires the alignment

\langle\varphi_{T}\rangle=(v_{T},\,0,\,0)\,,

the set of group elements that leaves it invariant is

\{e,\,T,\,T^{2}\}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.
By contrast, for the alignment

\langle\varphi_{S}\rangle=(v_{S},\,v_{S},\,v_{S})\,,

the invariant set is
\{e,\,S\}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Thus, this choice of vacuum alignments induces different residual symmetries in the
charged-lepton and neutrino sectors (
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, respectively),
which is precisely the pattern compatible with tri-bimaximal models.

A distinctive feature of the
A_{4}
setup is that its three inequivalent singlet
representations map uniquely to the three charged-lepton flavors. If one interchanges the
assignments of
\mu^{c}
and
\tau^{c}
, i.e. takes

e^{c}\sim\mathbf{1}
,
\mu^{c}\sim\mathbf{1^{\prime}}
,
\tau^{c}\sim\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}
,
then after electroweak and flavor breaking, the charged-lepton Yukawa terms read

\mathcal{L}_{\ell}\;=\;v_{T}\,\frac{v_{d}}{\Lambda}\,\bigl(y_{e}\,e^{c}e\;+\;y_{\mu}\,\mu^{c}\tau\;+\;y_{\tau}\,\tau^{c}\mu\bigr)\,,

(A.32)

which correspond to the mass matrix

m^{\prime}_{\ell}\;=\;v_{T}\,\frac{v_{d}}{\Lambda}\begin{pmatrix}y_{e}&0&0\\
0&0&y_{\tau}\\
0&y_{\mu}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad m^{\prime}_{\ell}m_{\ell}^{\prime\dagger}\;=\;v_{T}^{\,2}\,\frac{v_{d}^{\,2}}{\Lambda^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}|y_{e}|^{2}&0&0\\
0&|y_{\tau}|^{2}&0\\
0&0&|y_{\mu}|^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.33)

Consider first the diagonalization by multiplying on the
right
with the
permutation matrix

P\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(A.34)

This gives

m^{\prime}_{\ell,\mathrm{diag}}\;=\;m^{\prime}_{\ell}P\;=\;v_{T}\,\frac{v_{d}}{\Lambda}\begin{pmatrix}y_{e}&0&0\\
0&y_{\tau}&0\\
0&0&y_{\mu}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.35)

While the PMNS matrix is unaffected by this column permutation,
one should note that in this option the parameter
y_{\mu}
actually furnishes the

\tau
mass and
y_{\tau}
the
\mu
mass (the right-handed fields are permuted).

Alternatively, diagonalizing by multiplying on the left with

P^{\dagger}
yields

m^{\prime}_{\ell,\mathrm{diag}}\;=\;P^{\dagger}m^{\prime}_{\ell}\;=\;v_{T}\,\frac{v_{d}}{\Lambda}\begin{pmatrix}y_{e}&0&0\\
0&y_{\mu}&0\\
0&0&y_{\tau}\end{pmatrix},

(A.36)

which realizes the conventional identification

m_{e}\propto y_{e}
,
m_{\mu}\propto y_{\mu}
, and
m_{\tau}\propto y_{\tau}
.
Although the second option restores the conventional identification of the
charged-lepton masses, the PMNS matrix is correspondingly permuted. In
particular,

U^{\prime}_{\mathrm{HPS}}\;=\;P^{\dagger}\,U_{\mathrm{HPS}}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{3}}&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&0\\
-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{6}}&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}&-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

(A.37)

Therefore, with the correct assignment, the Yukawa coefficients align with the
charged-lepton mass terms without spoiling the tri-bimaximal (TBM) approximation.

Appendix B
Mathematical Properties of
A_{4}
Modular Forms

B.1
Lattices and
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
basis changes

A two–dimensional lattice can be written as

\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{2},

(B.1)

where
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are two linearly independent basis vectors. In a
standard coordinate choice one may take
e_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
and
e_{2}=(0,1)^{T}
,
while more generally, for a torus with periods
\omega_{1,2}
one may take

e_{1}=(\omega_{1},0)^{T}
and
e_{2}=(0,\omega_{2})^{T}
.

The modular group
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
consists of integer matrices with unit
determinant,

SL(2,\mathbb{Z})=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\ \Big|\ a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{Z},\ ad-bc=1\right\}.

(B.2)

It acts on the lattice basis by linear recombination,

g\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}^{\prime}\\
e_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}ae_{1}+be_{2}\\
ce_{1}+de_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.3)

This change of basis has three important geometric properties:

1.

Area preservation.
The oriented area spanned by
(e_{1},e_{2})
is
preserved because

\det\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=1.

(B.4)

2.

Orientation preservation.
Since
\det g>0
, the ordered pair

(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
keeps the same (right–handed or left–handed) orientation as

(e_{1},e_{2})
.

3.

The lattice itself is unchanged.
Although the basis changes,
the set of lattice points is the same:

\Lambda^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}e_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e_{2}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}(ae_{1}+be_{2})\oplus\mathbb{Z}(ce_{1}+de_{2})=\Lambda.

(B.5)

Thus
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
relates different descriptions of the same lattice.

Property 3 can be understood equivalently as keeping the basis fixed but
transforming the integer coordinates of lattice vectors. Any lattice vector
can be decomposed as

v=(e_{1},e_{2})\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(B.6)

Under a basis change
(e_{1},e_{2})\to(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
with
e^{\prime}_{i}=ge_{i}
, the same
geometric vector
v
is represented by new coordinates
(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})
:

(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}^{\prime}\\
v_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=(e_{1},e_{2})\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.7)

Using
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})=(e_{1},e_{2})g^{T}
, one finds

\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}^{\prime}\\
v_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=(g^{T})^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad(g^{T})^{-1}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(B.8)

Since
(g^{T})^{-1}
is again an
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
matrix, we may relabel it
as
g
and write the coordinate action simply as

\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}^{\prime}\\
v_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=g\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad g\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(B.9)

The linear transformations of integer coordinates can be grouped by the
value of
\mathrm{tr}\,g
:

1.

Finite-order case (elliptic elements): discrete rotations around lattice points.

Typical generators are

\mathrm{tr}\,g=-2:\ g=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\quad\Rightarrow\quad(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})=(-v_{1},-v_{2})\ \text{(rotation by $180^{\circ}$)},

(B.10)

\mathrm{tr}\,g=-1:\ g=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\ \text{(counterclockwise rotation by $120^{\circ}$)},

(B.11)

\mathrm{tr}\,g=0:\ g=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ \text{(counterclockwise rotation by $90^{\circ}$)},

(B.12)

\mathrm{tr}\,g=1:\ g=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}\ \text{(counterclockwise rotation by $60^{\circ}$)}.

(B.13)

The transpose
g^{T}
generates the corresponding rotation in the opposite
direction.

2.

Parabolic case (
|\mathrm{tr}\,g|=2
with
g\neq\pm I
): lattice shear/translation.

A standard family is

T^{N}=\begin{pmatrix}1&N\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad T^{N}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}+Nv_{2}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(B.14)

with inverse

(T^{N})^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-N\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(B.15)

3.

Hyperbolic case (
|\mathrm{tr}\,g|>2
): stretching/compression.

These transformations rescale lattice directions (one expanded and the other
contracted) while preserving the lattice area.

As an explicit illustration, take an integer vector

v=\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
5\end{pmatrix},

(B.16)

and act with the
\mathrm{tr}\,g=-1
matrix

g=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix},

(B.17)

corresponding to a counterclockwise
120^{\circ}
rotation. Then

v^{\prime}=gv=\begin{pmatrix}-5\\
-4\end{pmatrix}.

(B.18)

For the hexagonal (or
A_{2}
) lattice, the inner product in the basis

(1,0)^{T},(0,1)^{T}
is encoded by the metric

G=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\tfrac{1}{2}\\
-\tfrac{1}{2}&1\end{pmatrix}.

(B.19)

For the matrix
g
above, one finds that it preserves this metric,

g^{T}Gg=G,

(B.20)

so the norm is invariant:

\langle v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle=v^{T}g^{T}Ggv=v^{T}Gv=\langle v,v\rangle=21.

(B.21)

The angle between
v
and
v^{\prime}
follows from

\cos\theta=\frac{\langle v^{\prime},v\rangle}{\sqrt{\langle v,v\rangle\,\langle v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\rangle}}=\frac{-21/2}{21}=-\frac{1}{2},

(B.22)

hence
\theta=120^{\circ}
, consistent with the geometric interpretation.

As discussed above, an element

g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

acts on a rank–two lattice either as a change of basis or as an active
transformation of lattice vectors. We now specialize to the complex torus,
for which the lattice can be parameterized by a complex modulus
\tau
.

Consider the lattice

\Lambda_{\tau}=\mathbb{Z}\,\omega_{1}+\mathbb{Z}\,\omega_{2}\subset\mathbb{C},

(B.23)

and choose a convenient basis such that
\omega_{2}=1
and
\omega_{1}=\tau
.
Equivalently, the period vector can be represented as

\begin{pmatrix}\omega_{1}\\
\omega_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tau\\
1\end{pmatrix},

(B.24)

so that
\Lambda_{\tau}=\mathbb{Z}\tau+\mathbb{Z}
. Acting with

g\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on the periods gives

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tau\\
1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\tau+b\\
c\tau+d\end{pmatrix}=(c\tau+d)\begin{pmatrix}\tau^{\prime}\\
1\end{pmatrix},\qquad\tau^{\prime}=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}.

(B.25)

Therefore the transformed lattice can be expressed as

\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}=\mathbb{Z}\tau^{\prime}+\mathbb{Z}\;\simeq\;(c\tau+d)^{-1}\Lambda_{\tau},

(B.26)

i.e.
\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}
differs from
\Lambda_{\tau}
only by a global complex
rescaling by
(c\tau+d)^{-1}
. The corresponding complex tori

T_{\tau}=\mathbb{C}/\Lambda_{\tau},\qquad T_{\tau^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}/\Lambda_{\tau^{\prime}}

(B.27)

are thus conformally equivalent: the modular transformation does not change
the intrinsic shape of the torus, but it rescales all lattice vectors by the
overall factor
(c\tau+d)^{-1}
.

This observation explains the appearance of the automorphy factor in modular
symmetry. If
\tau
is taken as the unique parameter describing the torus,
then any quantity that is sensitive to the overall scale of lattice vectors
must transform covariantly under
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. Concretely, for an
object
\Phi(\tau)
with modular weight
k
, modular invariance requires

\Phi(\tau)\;\longrightarrow\;\Phi(\tau^{\prime})=(c\tau+d)^{-k}\,\Phi(\tau),

(B.28)

so that the rescaling of the lattice is compensated by the factor

(c\tau+d)^{-k}
. Although the discussion above was phrased in terms of lattice
basis vectors, the same covariance applies to any vector on the torus, in
accordance with property 3 in the previous subsection.

B.2
Ring structure of
A_{4}
modular forms

The graded ring of holomorphic modular forms of level
\Gamma(3)
is
generated by
Y_{i}
modulo a single quadratic relation,

(YY)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}=Y_{2}^{2}+2\,Y_{1}Y_{3}\;=\;0.

(B.29)

Every modular form of weight
2n
can be expressed as a homogeneous polynomial of total
degree
n
in the variables
Y_{i}
, reduced using Eq. (
B.29
).

Weight 2.

There is only the triplet

M_{2}\cong\mathbf{3},\qquad Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}).

(B.30)

Weight 4.

From
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(\mathbf{3})
and (
B.29
):

\displaystyle M_{4}\cong\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbf{3},\qquad\begin{cases}Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}=Y_{1}^{2}+2Y_{2}Y_{3},\\
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}=Y_{3}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{2},\\
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}=Y_{2}^{2}+2Y_{1}Y_{3}\equiv 0,\\
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}=\bigl(Y_{1}^{2}-Y_{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{3}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{2},\;Y_{2}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{3}\bigr).\end{cases}

(B.31)

Weight 6.

From
\mathrm{Sym}^{3}(\mathbf{3})
and (
B.29
):

\displaystyle M_{6}\cong\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{3}\oplus\mathbf{3},\qquad\begin{cases}Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}}=Y_{1}^{3}+Y_{2}^{3}+Y_{3}^{3}-3Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3},\\[2.0pt]
Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}=\bigl(Y_{1}^{3}+2Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2}+2Y_{2}^{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{1}^{2}Y_{3}+2Y_{3}^{2}Y_{2}\bigr),\\[2.0pt]
Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}=\bigl(Y_{3}^{3}+2Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{3}^{2}Y_{1}+2Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2},\;Y_{3}^{2}Y_{2}+2Y_{2}^{2}Y_{1}\bigr),\\[2.0pt]
Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}c}=\bigl(Y_{2}^{3}+2Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{2}^{2}Y_{3}+2Y_{3}^{2}Y_{1},\;Y_{2}^{2}Y_{1}+2Y_{1}^{2}Y_{3}\bigr)\equiv 0.\end{cases}

(B.32)

For holomorphic vector-valued forms
F\in\mathcal{M}_{k}(\rho)
and

G\in\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})
, their pointwise product transforms with
weight
k+k^{\prime}
and representation
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}
.

It is convenient to collect all pairwise products via the linear map

\mu_{k,k^{\prime}}:\ \mathcal{M}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}}.

(B.33)

In general,
\mu_{k,k^{\prime}}
is not injective, and typically

\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k})\,\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k^{\prime}})>\dim(\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}})
.
Hence many product tensors lie in
\ker\mu_{k,k^{\prime}}
and should be rewritten as
linear combinations of a basis of
\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}}
.
Keeping both the original products and the corresponding linear combinations
produces an overcomplete, redundant set; in practice one expresses
results in terms of a chosen basis of
\mathcal{M}_{k+k^{\prime}}
.

For example, consider the case
\mathcal{M}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{4}
.
Let
Y\equiv Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}=(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})
and

X\equiv Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}=(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})=\bigl(Y_{1}^{2}-Y_{2}Y_{3},\;Y_{3}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{2},\;Y_{2}^{2}-Y_{1}Y_{3}\bigr).

(B.34)

All identities below are understood as equalities in the quotient ring by the constrain
Eq. (
B.29
).

(i)
\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{1}\to\mathbf{3}
.

Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\cdot Y\;=\;Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3a}}.

(B.35)

(ii)
\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{3}
.

Using
\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{3}\cong\mathbf{3}
as the fixed cyclic permutation

(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\mapsto(v_{2},v_{3},v_{1})
, one has

Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}\cdot Y\;\cong\;Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3b}}.

(B.36)

(iii)
\mathbf{3}\otimes\mathbf{3}\to\mathbf{1}\oplus\mathbf{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\mathbf{3}\oplus\mathbf{3}
.

Define the symmetric and antisymmetric triplets via the tensor products in the eigenbasis of
T
:

\displaystyle V_{s}=(Y\otimes X)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{s}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}2Y_{1}X_{1}-Y_{2}X_{3}-Y_{3}X_{2}\\
2Y_{3}X_{3}-Y_{1}X_{2}-Y_{2}X_{1}\\
2Y_{2}X_{2}-Y_{1}X_{3}-Y_{3}X_{1}\end{pmatrix},\qquad V_{a}=(Y\otimes X)_{\mathbf{3}_{\mathbf{a}}}=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{2}X_{3}-Y_{3}X_{2}\\
Y_{1}X_{2}-Y_{2}X_{1}\\
Y_{3}X_{1}-Y_{1}X_{3}\end{pmatrix}.

(B.37)

Then one has the explicit componentwise identities

\displaystyle(Y\otimes X)_{\mathbf{1}}

\displaystyle=Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}},

(B.38)

\displaystyle(Y\otimes X)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad(Y\otimes X)_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}=0,

\displaystyle V_{s}

\displaystyle=2\,Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3a}}-Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3b}},

\displaystyle V_{a}

\displaystyle=-\,Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3b}}+Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3c}}\;=\;-\,Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3b}}\quad\text{since }Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3c}}\equiv 0.

Thus every irrep component of

Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}^{\prime},\mathbf{3}}

is a linear combination of the weight-
6
basis

\{Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}},Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3a}},Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3b}}\}
;
the
\mathbf{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}
projections vanish identically.

If one replaces holomorphic modular forms by (harmonic) Maaß forms,
the pointwise product still transforms with weight
k+k^{\prime}
, but it
generically fails to satisfy the Laplace equation
\Delta_{k+k^{\prime}}(fg)=0
.
Hence closure under products and the finite-basis reductions above are
specific to the holomorphic setting.

B.3
Constructing rules for
A_{4}
high weight modular forms with
k>2

For convenience, we divide the even modular weights into two congruence classes,

k=4m
and
k=4m+2
, with
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Before imposing the constraint in
Eq. (
2.65
), the corresponding dimensions are

\frac{(2m+1)(2m+2)}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{(2m+2)(2m+3)}{2}\,.

For
k=4m
, any singlet modular form can be written as

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{a}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{b}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{c}\,,

while a triplet takes the form

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\alpha}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\beta}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\gamma}Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\,.

Here all exponents are non-negative integers and satisfy the weight-counting
relations

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c

\displaystyle=4m\,,

(B.39)

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma+4

\displaystyle=4m\,.

In this case, the total number of possible combinations of
(a,b,c)

(corresponding to singlet structures) is
m(m+1)/2
, while the number of
possible combinations of
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
(corresponding to triplet
structures) is
(m+1)(m+2)/2
. The total dimension can be cross-checked against the
general counting and reads

3\cdot\frac{m(m+1)}{2}+\frac{(m+1)(m+2)}{2}=\frac{(2m+2)(2m+1)}{2}\,.

(B.40)

For
k=4m+2
, each singlet can be written as a monomial of the form

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{a}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{b}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{c}\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

while each triplet can be written as

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\alpha}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\beta}\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}\bigr)^{\gamma}Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\,.

The corresponding weight-counting relations read

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c+6

\displaystyle=4m+2\,,

(B.41)

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma+2

\displaystyle=4m+2\,.

The number of singlet structures is
m(m+1)/2
, while the number of triplet
structures is
(m+1)(m+2)/2
. Consequently, the total dimension is

\frac{m(m+1)}{2}+3\cdot\frac{(m+1)(m+2)}{2}=\frac{(2m+2)(2m+3)}{2}\,,

(B.42)

in agreement with the general counting formula.

Now impose the constraint in Eq. (
2.65
), namely

Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}=0
. In the tensor construction, this amounts to removing all
structures that contain at least one factor of
Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}
. In terms of
the exponents, this means
c\geq 1
or
\gamma\geq 1
. To count such contributions, we define
shifted variables
c^{\prime}=c-1
and
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma-1
. The relations
in Eq. (
B.39
) and Eq. (
B.41
) then become

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c^{\prime}

\displaystyle=4(m-1)\,,

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma^{\prime}+4

\displaystyle=4(m-1)\,,

and

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c^{\prime}+6

\displaystyle=4(m-1)+2\,,

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma^{\prime}+2

\displaystyle=4(m-1)+2\,.

This shows that the total dimension of all terms involving

Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}
coincides with the dimension of modular forms
with weight decreased by
4
. Of course, when
c=0
or
\gamma=0
,
the corresponding relations in terms of
c^{\prime}
or
\gamma^{\prime}
should not be included.

In terms of the generic weight parameter
k=2n
, the dimension formula

\frac{(n+2)(n+1)}{2}-(2n+1)=\frac{(n-2+2)(n-2+1)}{2}

simply expresses the fact that the difference between the unconstrained
and constrained dimensions at weight
k=2n
is equal to the dimension
of the modular forms at weight
k=2n-4
. Moreover, from the explicit
construction of symmetric tensors and the corresponding cancellation, we
see that, for modular weight
k=2n
, all symmetric tensors built from

(n-2)
copies of
Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}
must have their tensor products
with
Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}
removed.

As an explicit example, consider modular forms of weight
k=8
, i.e.
Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{r}}
with
m=2
. The total dimension is

\frac{(2m+2)(2m+1)}{2}=\frac{(2\cdot 2+2)(2\cdot 2+1)}{2}=15=6+3\cdot 3\,,

which corresponds to six singlets,

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)},\qquad Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)},\qquad Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)},

\displaystyle Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)},\qquad Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)},\qquad Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}\,,

and three triplets,

Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}=Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(4)},\quad Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}=Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(4)},\quad Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}c}=Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(4)}\,.

Under the constraint in Eq. (
2.65
), the contributions
involving
Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}
must be removed. In particular, the
singlet combinations

Y_{\mathbf{1}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)},\quad Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)},\quad Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}

and the triplet

Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}c}=Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(4)}

are precisely of the form

\bigl(Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}\otimes Y_{\mathbf{3}}^{(2)}\bigr)_{\mathrm{sym}}\otimes Y_{\mathbf{1}^{\prime\prime}}^{(4)}

and are therefore eliminated by the constraint.

Examples.

We record the explicit modular forms used in our analysis.

Weight 4.

From the symmetric square of the triplet
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(B.43)

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0,

\displaystyle Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}}\,.

Weight 6.

Using the exponent relations in Eq. (
B.41
) with
m=1
,

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma

\displaystyle=4\,,

whose solutions are

a=b=c=0,\qquad(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\{(1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1)\}.

The resulting modular forms are

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{1}}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(B.44)

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}a}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}b}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle Y^{(6)}_{\mathbf{3}c}=\bigl(Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0\,.

Weight 8.

Using the relations in Eq. (
B.39
) with
m=2
,

\displaystyle 4a+4b+4c

\displaystyle=8,

\displaystyle 4\alpha+4\beta+4\gamma

\displaystyle=4\,,

with solutions

\displaystyle(a,b,c)\in\{(2,0,0),(0,2,0),(0,0,2),(1,1,0),(1,0,1),(0,1,1)\},

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\{(1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1)\}.

Thus there are six singlets and three triplets before imposing the constraint.
They are

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}a}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}},

(B.45)

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}a}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}},

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}a}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}b}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}b}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{1}b}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{1}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0,

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}a}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}b}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}},

\displaystyle Y^{(8)}_{\mathbf{3}c}=\bigl(Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\bigr)_{\mathbf{3}}\;\overset{\eqref{eq:modular_constrain}}{=}\;0\,.

In particular, the four structures containing
Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}
vanish upon
imposing the constraint in Eq. (
2.65
).

C.2
Holomorphy and SUSY UV matching for modular-invariant SMEFT operators

In general
\mathcal{N}=1
SUSY, superspace holomorphy refers to whether an interaction originates from a chiral superspace integral (an
F
-term) rather than a full superspace integral (a
D
-term).
Here
\Phi
denotes a chiral superfield and
V
the gauge vector superfield.
Superpotential couplings and gauge–kinetic terms take the schematic form

\int d^{2}\theta\,W(\Phi)\,,\qquad\int d^{2}\theta\,f_{ab}(\Phi)\,W^{a\alpha}W^{b}_{\alpha}\,,

(C.1)

where
W(\Phi)
and
f_{ab}(\Phi)
are holomorphic functions of chiral superfields
\Phi
.
By contrast, Kähler and more general kinetic structures arise from

\int d^{4}\theta\,K(\Phi,\bar{\Phi};V)\,,

(C.2)

with
K
a real function of
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\dagger}
.
Specifically, the
\mathcal{N}=1
SUSY action can be written as

S\;=\;\int d^{4}x\,d^{2}\theta\,d^{2}\bar{\theta}\;K\!\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi};V\right)\;+\;\left[\int d^{4}x\,d^{2}\theta\;\left(W(\Phi)+\frac{1}{4}\,f_{ab}(\Phi)\,W^{a\alpha}W^{b}{}_{\alpha}\right)\;+\;\text{h.c.}\right].

(C.3)

In a modular-invariant setup, the modulus
\tau
enters F-terms by holomorphic modular forms, while D-terms are generically non-holomorphic and may depend on both
\tau
and
\tau^{*}
. For a renormalizable SUSY action,
f_{ab}
is taken to be constant, and the superpotential can be written schematically as

W(\Phi)=Y(\tau)^{k_{W}}\,\varphi^{(I_{1})}\cdots\varphi^{(I_{n})}\,,\qquad k_{W}=\sum_{a=1}^{n}k_{I_{a}}\,.

(C.4)

For the Kähler potential, we have

K(\Phi,\bar{\Phi},V)=\frac{1}{\left\langle-i\tau+i\bar{\tau}\right\rangle^{k_{K}}}\,K(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},V)\;+\Delta K\;,

(C.5)

in which

\Delta K\;=\;\sum_{k,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\,\alpha_{k,\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\;\big\langle i\bar{\tau}-i\tau\big\rangle^{\,k-k_{K}}\,\Big[\big(Y^{(k)}{Y^{*}}^{(k)}\big)_{\mathbf{r}}\;K(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},V)_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\Big]_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

(C.6)

where
k
is any even integer satisfying
k\geq 2
.

Here, we focus directly on the modular SUSY UV matching mechanisms that generate the modular-invariant SMEFT operators discussed in the main text, and we use the F- and D-term language only to label the UV origin of the relevant couplings.

We impose the MFV-like assumptions used in the conclusions that
\tau
-dependence in the EFT is restricted to (anti-)holomorphic modular forms, and heavy mediator masses are taken to be
\tau
-independent constants. Accordingly, the modular dressing of an SMEFT operator structure
\mathcal{O}
can be written schematically as

\mathcal{O}\ \longrightarrow\ \bigl[\,Y^{(k)}_{\mathbf{r}}(\tau)\,Y^{(k^{\prime})*}_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}(\tau)\,\mathcal{O}\,\bigr]_{\mathbf{1}}\,,

(C.7)

with the tensor product taken in flavor space and projected to the singlet. In particular, these assumptions exclude modulus-dependent propagator denominators such as
1/M(\tau)^{2}
. They also motivate discarding kinetic-mediated contributions whenever modular covariance forces explicit
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)
-dependent prefactors to appear in covariant kinetic structures, since such prefactors would propagate into Wilson coefficients and lie outside the minimal dressing class (
C.7
).
Under these restriction, the SUSY UV kinetic term in modular-invariant SUSY UV completion is uniquely constrained to take the form

K_{\rm UV}=\sum_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\,\beta_{\mathbf{r},\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\;\Big[\big(Y^{(k_{K})}{Y^{*}}^{(k_{K})}\big)_{\mathbf{r}}\;K(\varphi,\bar{\varphi},V)_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}\Big]_{\mathbf{1}}\,.

(C.8)

In this setup, we discuss which common tree-level and loop-level exchange mechanisms in a modular-invariant SUSY UV completion can generate these modular-invariant SMEFT operators
Our organization follows the tree-level UV/IR dictionary in Refs.
[
103
,
104
]
.
A compact summary is collected in Table
10
.

Two-fermion classes.

At the level of SUSY UV matching, the Weinberg operator
(LH)(LH)
is the standard example generated at tree level by exchanging a heavy chiral supermultiplet through holomorphic superpotential couplings (seesaw-type mediators), i.e. via the exchange of its scalar or fermionic component in components language. In this case the modular dependence of the Wilson coefficient is inherited from the holomorphic modular-form dependence of the superpotential couplings, while the heavy masses are
\tau
-independent by assumption.

The two-fermion classes
\psi^{2}H^{3}
and
\psi^{2}H^{2}D
can also be generated at tree level.
The holomorphic class
\psi^{2}H^{3}
is most naturally induced by integrating out heavy scalars through holomorphic superpotential couplings.
By contrast, the non-holomorphic class
\psi^{2}H^{2}D
admits two standard tree-level realizations: heavy-vector exchange via current couplings, which is rooted in UV kinetic/Kähler structures and is therefore “D-term-like” in origin; and heavy vectorlike-fermion exchange, corresponding to exchanging the fermionic component of a heavy (vectorlike) chiral supermultiplet through holomorphic superpotential couplings, i.e. a UV F-term origin.
Under our MFV-like assumption, matching contributions from both the F-term and D-term sectors are allowed,
and for D-term–induced operators the
\tau
-dependence of the Wilson coefficients typically appears in the mixed form

Y(\tau)\,Y^{*}(\tau)
.

Finally, dipole operators
\psi^{2}XH
are chirality-holomorphic.
In renormalizable SUSY UV completions, they are typically generated only at one loop.
From the UV perspective, the matching necessarily requires holomorphic superpotential Yukawa/trilinear couplings (F-term) together with gauge interactions (D-term/kinetic), and the heavy states running in the loop typically include charged/colored heavy fermions and heavy scalars (and their superpartners).
Here the
D
-term/kinetic vertices should couple to modular factors in a holomorphic way; otherwise the matching would introduce intrinsically non-holomorphic couplings, which would in turn require a nontrivial (and model-dependent) choice of modular-weight assignments to restore modular covariance.
Under our minimal modular-spurion setup, the
\tau
-dependence of the resulting Wilson coefficients is carried by modular forms appearing in the superpotential couplings and gauge interactions.

Four-fermion classes.

For baryon-number conserving four-fermion operators, a useful UV-level distinction is between current–current structures and operators of the
(\overline{L}R)(\overline{R}L)+\mathrm{h.c.}
type.
Current–current operators such as
(\overline{L}L)(\overline{L}L)
,
(\overline{R}R)(\overline{R}R)
and
(\overline{L}L)(\overline{R}R)
are readily generated at tree level by heavy-vector exchange through couplings to fermion currents; from the UV viewpoint these couplings are naturally rooted in kinetic/Kähler structures and are therefore D-term-like in origin.
Operators of the
(\overline{L}R)(\overline{R}L)+\mathrm{h.c.}
type can also arise from heavy-vector exchange, possibly after Fierz rearrangements, depending on the mediator quantum numbers.
Both types of non-holomorphic four-fermion classes may also be produced by exchanging heavy chiral multiplets through holomorphic superpotential interactions (typically via heavy-scalar exchange), in which case the underlying UV couplings are holomorphic but the resulting four-fermion operator appears in the low-energy EFT with a non-holomorphic structure.
These are precisely the cases in which mixed modular dependence of the form
Y(\tau)Y^{*}(\tau)
is expected and is consistent with Eq. (
C.7
).

By contrast, the schematic class
(\overline{L}R)(\overline{L}R)
(such as
\mathcal{O}^{(1,8)}_{quqd}
and
\mathcal{O}^{(1,3)}_{lequ}
) is holomorphic in the chirality-based classification.
In SUSY-UV interpretation, generating this structure most naturally requires a tree-level heavy-scalar exchange.
Equivalently, in a supersymmetric completion this corresponds to exchanging the scalar component of a heavy chiral supermultiplet, with the required renormalizable trilinear couplings arising from holomorphic superpotential interactions (an F-term origin).
Under our minimal modular-spurion setup, the modulus dependence in these trilinear couplings is carried only by holomorphic modular forms, so that the resulting Wilson coefficient inherits a purely holomorphic modular-form factor.

Meanwhile, the dimension-six baryon-number violating (BNV) operators can be accommodated within the same SUSY-UV matching logic, but with baryon-number violating superpotential couplings.
Concretely, their minimal realization most economically proceeds via a tree-level heavy-scalar exchange, so that the effective four-fermion interaction arises after integrating out the exchanged scalar.
Equivalently, the required renormalizable trilinear vertices originate from holomorphic superpotential interactions (an F-term origin).
Under our minimal modular-spurion assumptions, any
\tau
-dependence enters only through holomorphic modular forms appearing in these superpotential trilinears (with
\tau
-independent mediator masses), and the resulting Wilson coefficients inherit the corresponding holomorphic modular-form factors.

non-holomorphic

Tree level:

UV D-term (heavy vector) or/and

UV F-term (heavy scalar)

Dim-6:

(\overline{L}L)(\overline{R}R)

Summary of modular SUSY UV matching mechanisms for the fermionic (dimension-five and dimension-six) operator classes in the modular-invariant SMEFT.

C.3
Some dimension-7 modular invariant operators

At dimension-
7
, we present the modular-invariant operator structures corresponding to the bilinear SMEFT operators that do not involve field-strength tensors, namely the classes
\phi^{2}H^{4}
,
\phi^{2}H^{3}D
, and
\phi^{2}H^{2}D^{2}
.

•

\phi^{2}H^{4}
and
\phi^{2}H^{2}D^{2}
:

The associated operators are

\mathcal{O}_{LH}=\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}(L^{i}_{L}CL_{L}^{m})H^{j}H^{n}(H^{\dagger}H),

(C.9)

\mathcal{O}_{LHD}^{(1)}=\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}L^{i}_{L}C(D^{\mu}L^{j}_{L})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n}),

(C.10)

\mathcal{O}_{LHD}^{(2)}=\epsilon_{im}\epsilon_{jn}L^{i}_{L}C(D^{\mu}L^{j}_{L})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n}).

(C.11)

For these classes, the corresponding modular-invariant structures obtained from the Hilbert series are

\displaystyle h^{3}\,h^{\dagger}\,l^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})+h^{3}\,h^{\dagger}\,l^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})+h^{3}\,h^{\dagger}\,l^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})

(C.12)

\displaystyle+2\,h^{2}\,l^{2}\,D^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})+2\,h^{2}\,l^{2}\,D^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})+2\,h^{2}\,l^{2}\,D^{2}\,Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e}).

One can see that this structure is almost identical to the construction of the dimension-
5
Weinberg operator. The last three terms, each with coefficient
2
, correspond to the two distinct operator structures that arise from the
\phi^{2}H^{2}D^{2}
class. Following the construction shown in Eq. (
4.2
), their explicit forms can be written down in a straightforward way. For instance, the operator

\mathcal{O}_{LHD}^{(1)}=\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}L_{L}^{i}C(D^{\mu}L_{L}^{j})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n})

corresponds to the
A_{4e}
-invariant structures

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}L^{i}_{L}C(D^{\mu}L^{j}_{L})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n})\right]_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

(C.13)

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}L^{i}_{L}C(D^{\mu}L^{j}_{L})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n})\right]_{\mathbf{1}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}L^{i}_{L}C(D^{\mu}L^{j}_{L})H^{m}(D_{\mu}H^{n})\right]_{\mathbf{1^{\prime\prime}}}\otimes Y^{(4)}_{\mathbf{1^{\prime}}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}}.

•

\phi^{2}H^{3}D
:

The associated operator is

\mathcal{O}_{LHDe}=\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}(L^{i}_{L}C\gamma_{\mu}E_{R})H^{j}H^{m}D^{\mu}H^{n}.

(C.14)

The corresponding modular-invariant structures obtained from the Hilbert series are

\displaystyle e\,h^{3}\,l\,D\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})+h^{3}\,l\,\mu\,D\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})+h^{3}\,l\,D\,\tau\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e}).

(C.15)

Similarly, these terms directly lead to the corresponding invariant operator structures,

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}(L^{i}_{L}C\gamma_{\mu}e_{R})H^{j}H^{m}D^{\mu}H^{n}\right]_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

(C.16)

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}(L^{i}_{L}C\gamma_{\mu}\mu_{R})H^{j}H^{m}D^{\mu}H^{n}\right]_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}},

\displaystyle\left[\left[\epsilon_{ij}\epsilon_{mn}(L^{i}_{L}C\gamma_{\mu}\tau_{R})H^{j}H^{m}D^{\mu}H^{n}\right]_{\mathbf{3}}\otimes Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}(\tau_{e})\right]_{\mathbf{1}}.

Appendix D
Non-holomorphic modular forms in
A_{4}
modular symmetry

In this appendix we summarize the ingredients needed for non-holomorphic (polyharmonic Maaß) modular forms at level
N=3
. We first collect the basic differential operators and their modular properties, and then list explicit
q
-expansions for the multiplets with weights
k<2
. For
k>2
, all structures coincide with the holomorphic case and are summarized in Appendix
B.2
.
At weight
k=2
, the polyharmonic Maaß sector contains the
A_{4}
singlet
\widehat{E}_{2}(\tau)
in addition to the triplet
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}}
; we denote this singlet by
Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{1}}\equiv\widehat{E}_{2}(\tau)
.

D.1
Differential operators
D
and
\xi

We adopt the standard Maaß–Shimura operators

D\;=\;\frac{1}{2\pi i}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\,,\qquad\xi_{k}\;=\;2i\,y^{k}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\tau}}\,,\qquad y=\mathrm{Im}\,\tau\,.

(D.1)

Let
Y(\tau)
be a polyharmonic Maaß form of weight
k
and level
N
. Then

\begin{aligned} \bigl(D^{1-k}Y\bigr)(\gamma\tau)&=(c\tau+d)^{2-k}\,\bigl(D^{1-k}Y\bigr)(\tau),\\
\bigl(\xi_{k}Y\bigr)(\gamma\tau)&=(c\tau+d)^{2-k}\,\bigl(\xi_{k}Y\bigr)(\tau),\end{aligned}\qquad\gamma=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma(N).

(D.2)

Hence both
D^{\,1-k}
and
\xi_{k}
map (polyharmonic) weight-
k
forms to weight-
2-k
modular forms.
Using the Fourier–Whittaker basis (
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
), one has for
n>0
:

D^{\,1-k}(q^{n})=n^{\,1-k}q^{n},\qquad D^{\,1-k}\!\left(y^{1-k}\right)=(-4\pi)^{k-1}(1-k)!,\qquad D^{\,1-k}\!\bigl(\Gamma(1-k,4\pi ny)\,q^{-n}\bigr)=0,

(D.3)

and

\xi_{k}(q^{n})=0,\qquad\xi_{k}\!\left(y^{1-k}\right)=1-k,\qquad\xi_{k}\!\bigl(\Gamma(1-k,4\pi ny)\,q^{-n}\bigr)=-(4\pi n)^{1-k}q^{-n}.

(D.4)

Because level-
N
holomorphic modular forms do not exist at negative weight, nontrivial polyharmonic Maaß forms necessarily satisfy
k\leq 2
.

D.2
Level
A_{4}
: explicit series for
k\leq 2

The non-holomorphic multiplets of weights
k=0,-2,-4
can be obtained by acting with
D
and
\xi_{k}
on holomorphic forms of higher weight. Below we collect convenient representatives of their
q
-expansions; dots indicate higher-order terms that follow the same pattern.

Weight
k=0
triplet.

There exists a non-holomorphic weight-
0
triplet
Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}=(Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},1},Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},2},Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},3})^{T}
and the trivial singlet
Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{1}}=1
such that

\xi_{0}\!\bigl(Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)=\Omega\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}},\qquad D\!\bigl(Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3}}\bigr)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\,Y^{(2)}_{\mathbf{3}},

(D.5)

where
\Omega
is an intertwiner implementing the equivalence between the real triplet and its complex conjugate.
A convenient choice of
q
-expansions is

\displaystyle Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},1}\;=\;

\displaystyle y-\frac{3\,e^{-4\pi y}}{\pi\,q}-\frac{9\,e^{-8\pi y}}{2\pi\,q^{2}}-\frac{e^{-12\pi y}}{\pi\,q^{3}}-\frac{21\,e^{-16\pi y}}{4\pi\,q^{4}}-\frac{18\,e^{-20\pi y}}{5\pi\,q^{5}}-\frac{3\,e^{-24\pi y}}{2\pi\,q^{6}}+\cdots

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\frac{9\log 3}{4\pi}-\frac{3q}{\pi}-\frac{9q^{2}}{2\pi}-\frac{q^{3}}{\pi}-\frac{21q^{4}}{4\pi}-\frac{18q^{5}}{5\pi}-\frac{3q^{6}}{2\pi}+\cdots\,,

(D.6)

\displaystyle Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},2}\;=\;

\displaystyle\frac{27\,q^{1/3}e^{\pi y/3}}{\pi}\left(\frac{e^{-3\pi y}}{4q}+\frac{e^{-7\pi y}}{5q^{2}}+\frac{5\,e^{-11\pi y}}{16q^{3}}+\frac{2\,e^{-15\pi y}}{11q^{4}}+\frac{2\,e^{-19\pi y}}{7q^{5}}+\frac{4\,e^{-23\pi y}}{17q^{6}}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\frac{9\,q^{1/3}}{2\pi}\left(1+\frac{7q}{4}+\frac{8q^{2}}{7}+\frac{9q^{3}}{5}+\frac{14q^{4}}{13}+\frac{31q^{5}}{16}+\frac{20q^{6}}{19}+\cdots\right)\!,

(D.7)

\displaystyle Y^{(0)}_{\mathbf{3},3}\;=\;

\displaystyle\frac{9\,q^{2/3}e^{2\pi y/3}}{2\pi}\left(\frac{e^{-2\pi y}}{q}+\frac{7\,e^{-6\pi y}}{4q^{2}}+\frac{8\,e^{-10\pi y}}{7q^{3}}+\frac{9\,e^{-14\pi y}}{5q^{4}}+\frac{14\,e^{-18\pi y}}{13q^{5}}+\frac{31\,e^{-22\pi y}}{16q^{6}}+\cdots\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\frac{27\,q^{2/3}}{\pi}\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{q}{5}+\frac{5q^{2}}{16}+\frac{2q^{3}}{11}+\frac{2q^{4}}{7}+\frac{9q^{5}}{17}+\frac{21q^{6}}{20}+\cdots\right)\!.

(D.8)

Weight
k=-2
multiplets from weight
4
.

Acting with

(or

4

q

\displaystyle\;+\frac{\pi\,\zeta(3)}{40\,\zeta(4)}+\frac{21\,q}{8\pi^{3}}+\frac{189\,q^{2}}{64\pi^{3}}+\frac{169\,q^{3}}{72\pi^{3}}+\frac{1533\,q^{4}}{512\pi^{3}}+\frac{1323\,q^{5}}{500\pi^{3}}+\frac{169\,q^{6}}{64\pi^{3}}+\cdots,

\displaystyle-\,\frac{81\,q^{1/3}}{16\pi^{3}}\left(1+\frac{73\,q}{64}+\frac{344\,q^{2}}{343}+\frac{567\,q^{3}}{500}+\frac{20198\,q^{4}}{2197}+\frac{4681\,q^{5}}{4096}+\cdots\right),

\displaystyle\;-\frac{729\,q^{2/3}}{8\pi^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{7\,q}{125}+\frac{65\,q^{2}}{1024}+\frac{74\,q^{3}}{1331}+\cdots\right).

(D.18)
